<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{main thm}
Let $\Sigma$ be a connected orientable surface of finite type without boundary and with genus $g\ge 0$ and $p\ge 0$ marked points such that $3g-3+p\geq 2$. 

Then, any measurable embedding coupling from $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ to $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ is a measure equivalence coupling.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cr biembedability}
    Let $\Sigma$ be a connected orientable surface of finite type without boundary and with genus $g\ge 0$ and $p\ge 0$ marked points such that $3g-3+p\geq 2$.

    If a countable group $H$ measurably embeds into $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ and $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ measurably embeds into $H$, then $H$ is virtually isomorphic to $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ up to finite kernels.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cr OE rigidity}Let $\Sigma$ be a connected orientable surface of finite type without boundary and with genus $g\ge 0$ and $p\ge 0$ marked points such that $3g-3+p\geq 2$. Let $G$ and $H$ be two copies of $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$. Assume that there exist free probability measure-preserving actions of $G$ and $H$ on a standard probability space $X$ such that for almost every $x\in X$, $H\cdot x\subseteq G\cdot x$. 
 
Then, for almost every $x\in X$, $H\cdot x=G\cdot x$. If additionally the actions are ergodic, then they are virtually conjugate.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cr monotonicity of complexity}
    Let $\Sigma$ and $\Sigma'$ be connected orientable surfaces without boundary and of finite type. If $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ measurably embeds into $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma')$, then ${\kappa(\Sigma)\leq \kappa(\Sigma')}$.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- def-1: body_md -->
\label{def mapping class group, subgroups of subsurfaces, modz def}
    Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item The \textit{mapping class group} of $\Sigma$ consists of isotopy classes of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of $\Sigma$: $$\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma) = \mathrm{Diffeo^+}(\Sigma)/\,\mathrm{isotopy}~.$$
        An element $f\in\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ is called a \textit{mapping class}.
        \item The \textit{extended mapping class group} of $\Sigma$ is$$\mathrm{Mod}^{\pm}(\Sigma) = \mathrm{Diffeo}(\Sigma)/\,\mathrm{isotopy}$$
        where $\mathrm{Diffeo}(\Sigma)$ consists of diffeomorphisms of $\Sigma$ that preserve or invert the orientation. The group $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ is an index $2$ subgroup of $\mathrm{Mod}^{\pm}(\Sigma)$.
        \item Define $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ as the subgroup of $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ acting trivially on the first homology group of $\Sigma$ modulo 3, i.e.\ 
        $$\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)=\ker\big[\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{H}_1(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}))\big]~.$$
        \item Let $Q$ be a subsurface of $\Sigma$. Then, $\mathrm{Mod}_{ Q }(\Sigma)$ is the subgroup of $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ consisting of the mapping classes of $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ that are identity outside of the closure $\bar Q\subseteq \Sigma$ of the subsurface $Q$ of $\Sigma$.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end def-1 -->
<!-- th-1: body_md -->
\label{modz th}
    Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface with $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 1$.
    
    Then, $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ is a finite-index torsion-free subgroup of $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ consisting of only pure mapping classes.
<!-- end th-1 -->
<!-- rem-1: body_md -->
Ivanov works on compact surfaces. When the surface has marked points or boundary components, the latter theorem can be deduced from the Handel--Mosher theorem \cite{handel_subgroup_2020}, by noticing that in this case $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ has a well-behaved map to the outer automorphism group of some free group.
<!-- end rem-1 -->
<!-- th-2: body_md -->
\label{th ivanov}
Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface with $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 2$.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep, nolistsep]
    \item If $\kappa(\Sigma)=2$, then $\mathrm{Mod}^{\pm}(\Sigma) \longrightarrow \mathrm{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ has finite index image and finite kernel.
    \item If $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 3$, then $\mathrm{Mod}^{\pm}(\Sigma) \simeq \mathrm{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ is an isomorphism.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end th-2 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{denh twist prop} Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface.
    \begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, nolistsep]
        \item \label{intersection formula dehn twists}\cite[Proposition~3.2]{farb_primer_2012} For all $a,b\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ and $k\in\mathbb{Z}$,
        $$i(T_a^k(b),b)=|k|\,i(a,b)^2~.$$
        \item \label{dehn twists injectivity}\cite[Fact~3.9]{farb_primer_2012} In particular, Dehn twists have infinite order and for every $a,b\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ and $m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}$,
        $$T_a^m=T_{b}^n\iff a=b\text{ and }m=n~.$$
        \item \label{dehn twists squared give free groups}\cite[Theorem~3.14]{farb_primer_2012} If $i(a,b)\geq 2$, then $T_a$ and $T_b$ generate $F_2$. If $i(a,b)=1$ and $j,k$ are such that $|j|,|k|\geq 2$, then $T_a^j$ and $T_b^k$ generate $F_2$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{nonsep dehn twists are not in modz}
    Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface and let $T_a$ be a Dehn twist around a nonseparating curve $a$. If $T_a^k\in \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$, then $|k|\geq 2$.
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The curve $a$ being nonseparating, there exists a curve $b$ on $\Sigma$ such that $i(a,b)=1$. Then, the homology classes $[a]$ and $[b]$ can be completed into a basis of $\mathrm{H}_1(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z})$ and by \cite[Proposition~6.3]{farb_primer_2012}, 
    $$T_a\cdot[b]=[a]+[b]\text{ and }T_a^{-1}\cdot[b]=[a]-[b]~.$$
    Thus, both $T_a$ and $T_a^{-1}$ act nontrivially on $\mathrm{H}_1(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z})$ so they are not in $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- cr-1: body_md -->
\label{prop in modz all twists are free groups}
    Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface. For every $a,b$ curves such that $i(a,b)\geq 1$, ${\langle {T_a} \rangle\cap \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)}$ and $\langle {T_b} \rangle\cap \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ generate a nonabelian free group.
<!-- end cr-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
When $i(a,b)\geq 2$, $T_a$ and $T_b$ generate a nonabelian free group by Point~\ref{dehn twists squared give free groups} of Proposition~\ref{denh twist prop}. When $i(a,b)=1$, by Proposition~\ref{nonsep dehn twists are not in modz}, $\langle {T_a} \rangle\cap \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)=\langle {T_a^j} \rangle$ and $\langle {T_b} \rangle\cap \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)=\langle {T_b^k} \rangle$ for some $|j|,|k|\geq 2$. By Point~\ref{dehn twists squared give free groups} of Proposition~\ref{denh twist prop}, $\langle {T_a^j,T_b^k} \rangle\simeq F_2$ a nonabelian free group of two generators.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- def-2: body_md -->
\label{def dual graph}
    Let $\sigma$ be a multicurve of a connected surface $\Sigma$. The graph dual to $\sigma$, denoted $\Gamma_{\sigma}=(E,V)$, is defined as follows.
    \begin{itemize}[noitemsep, nolistsep, label=-]
        \item The vertices $V$ correspond to closures of connected components of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma$.
        \item The edges $E$ correspond to curves of $\sigma$.
        \item A curve $c\in \sigma$ connects as an edge the two vertices of $V$ that correspond to the closures of components of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma$ that share $c$ in their boundary.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end def-2 -->
<!-- rem-2: body_md -->
\label{rem equation star}
    The graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is finite, non oriented and can have loops. Each vertex $v$ is a surface with some topological complexity $\kappa(v)$. Recall that the topological complexity is the number of curves needed for a pants decomposition. Therefore, 
    $$\kappa(\Sigma)=\#E+\sum_{v\in V} \kappa(v)~.$$
    In other words, if we label each vertex $v$ by $\kappa(v)$ and each edge by $1$, then the complexity of the whole surface is simply the sum of all labels.
    Now, denote by $b_1$ the number of edges outside of a covering tree of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$. Then,
    $$\#V-\#E=1-b_1~.$$
    This gives us the following useful equation on the dual graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}$:
    \begin{equation*}\label{equation star}
        \kappa(\Sigma)+1=2\#V+\sum_{v\in V}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right) + b_1~.\tag{$\bigstar$}
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end rem-2 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lemma n(g,p)}

Fix a connected surface $\Sigma$ with complexity $\kappa(\Sigma)\ge 1$. Let 
$${n_{\Sigma}}=\left\lfloor\frac{\kappa(\Sigma)+1}{2}\right\rfloor~.$$
Let $\sigma$ be a multicurve of $\Sigma$. Let $\Gamma_{\sigma}=(V,E)$ be the graph dual to $\sigma$. Assume that for every component $v\in V$, $\kappa(v)\ge 1$. 

Then, $\#V\leq {n_{\Sigma}}$. Moreover, if $\#V={n_{\Sigma}}$ then the decomposition has the following form.
\begin{itemize}
    \item When $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is odd, $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is a tree and each component of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma$ has complexity $1$.
    \item When $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is even, the decomposition has exactly one of the two following forms.
    \begin{itemize}[noitemsep, nolistsep]
        \item The graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is a tree with exactly one component of complexity $2$ and all the other components have complexity~$1$.
        \item The graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ has exactly one edge outside a covering tree, and all components have complexity~$1$.
    \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Recall (\ref{equation star}): 
        $$\kappa(\Sigma)+1=2\#V+\sum_{v\in V}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right) + b_1~.$$
    On one hand, all the $(\kappa(v)-1)$ and $b_1$ are positive or null so 
    $$\#V\leq \left\lfloor\frac{\kappa(\Sigma)+1}{2}\right\rfloor={n_{\Sigma}}.$$

    On the other hand, to maximise $\#V$ when $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is odd, the only solution is to take $\kappa(v)=1$ for all $v$ and $b_1=0$, so we indeed get a tree of complexity $1$ components. When $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is even, either there is a cycle ($b_1=1$) and all components are complexity $1$ or it is a tree ($b_1=0$) and there is exactly one complexity $2$ component.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- rem-3: body_md -->
\label{rem si decompo est un arbre à petites composantes alors cest maximal}
    Recall that $\delta_{\Sigma}$ is defined so that ${n_{\Sigma}}=\left(\kappa(\Sigma)+1-\delta_{\Sigma}\right)/{2}$.
    If a decomposition multicurve $\sigma$ of $\Sigma$ verifies
    \begin{itemize}
        \item $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is a tree,
        \item $\sum_{v\in V(\Gamma_{\sigma})}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)=\delta_{\Sigma}$,
    \end{itemize}
    then (\ref{equation star}) gives
        $\kappa(\Sigma)+1=2\#V+\delta_{\Sigma}$
    so that $\#V={n_{\Sigma}}$. Thus $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ necessarily has ${n_{\Sigma}}$ vertices.
<!-- end rem-3 -->
<!-- ex-1: body_md -->
\label{exemple de decompo pour sphere n trous}
Let $\Sigma$ be an $n$-punctured sphere, i.e.\ a surface of type $\Sigma_{0,p,b}$ with ${b+p=n}$ and assume that $n\geq 4$. Let us build a decomposition $\sigma$ of $\Sigma$ by induction on $n$ such that $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is a tree with $$\sum_{v\in V(\Gamma_{\sigma})}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)=\delta_{\Sigma}~.$$ 
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $n\in\{4,5\}$, the decomposition multicurve is taken empty.
    \item If $n\geq 6$, take a curve $c$ of $\Sigma$ such that the connected components of $\Sigma\setminus c$ are a $4$-punctured sphere $S$ and an $(n-2)$-punctured sphere $\Sigma'$. Then, apply the induction hypothesis to $\Sigma'$ and get a decomposition multicurve $\sigma'$, as illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig decompo sphere n trous}. Notice that $\delta_{\Sigma}=\delta_{\Sigma'}$ so we have $$\sum_{v\in V(\Gamma_{\sigma'})}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)=\delta_{\Sigma}~.$$  Define $\sigma=\sigma'\sqcup\{c\}$ and notice that $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is the tree $\Gamma_{\sigma'}$ with an additional leaf corresponding to the $4$-punctured sphere $S$. The complexity of $S$ is $1$, so we still have $$\sum_{v\in V(\Gamma_{\sigma})}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)=0+\sum_{v\in V(\Gamma_{\sigma'})}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)=\delta_{\Sigma}~.$$
    \begin{figure}[ht]
	\centering
	\includesvg[width=0.6\textwidth]{313final}
    \caption{Induction step while constructing a $0$-maximal decomposition in Example~\ref{exemple de decompo pour sphere n trous}.}
	\label{fig decompo sphere n trous}
\end{figure}
\end{itemize}
Note that $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ has ${n_{\Sigma}}$ vertices by Remark~\ref{rem si decompo est un arbre à petites composantes alors cest maximal}. Thus, this construction illustrates Lemma~\ref{lemma n(g,p)} in the case of a tree dual graph for an $n$-punctured sphere.
<!-- end ex-1 -->
<!-- def-3: body_md -->
\label{def kmax decompo}
    A $k$-maximal decomposition of a connected surface $\Sigma$ is a multicurve $\sigma$ such that $\Sigma\setminus \sigma$ has ${n_{\Sigma}}-k$ connected components, of which exactly $2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$ components have complexity~$2$.
<!-- end def-3 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lemma structure of k max decopos} Let $\sigma$ be a $k$-maximal decomposition of a connected surface $\Sigma$.
    \begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, nolistsep]
        \item \label{point 1 lemma structure of k max decopos}The dual graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is a tree. The components that are not of complexity $2$ have complexity~$1$. 
        \item \label{point 2 lemma structure of k max decopos}There is no curve $c$ disjoint from $\sigma$ such that the multicurve $\sigma\sqcup\{c\}$ gives a decomposition with more connected components of complexity at least $1$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Denote $\Gamma_{\sigma}=(V,E)$ and recall (\ref{equation star}):
    $$\kappa(\Sigma)+1=2\#V+\sum_{v\in V}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right) + b_1~.$$
    By definition, 
    $$2{n_{\Sigma}}+\delta_{\Sigma}=\kappa(\Sigma)+1~.$$
    Note that $\#V={n_{\Sigma}}-k$ and $$\sum_v\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)\geq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$$
    which gives $$2\# V+\sum_v\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)\geq 2{n_{\Sigma}}-2k+2k+\delta_{\Sigma} =\kappa(\Sigma)+1~.$$
    Thus, the above inequality is an equality by equation (\ref{equation star}), which forces $b_1$ to be zero and $$\sum_v\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)=2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$$
    which means that all the other components have complexity~$1$. This proves Point~\ref{point 1 lemma structure of k max decopos}.

    If $c$ is a curve on a subsurface $S$ of complexity $1$ or $2$, then $S\setminus c$ has at most one component of positive complexity, which proves Point~\ref{point 1 lemma structure of k max decopos}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- def-4: body_md -->
\label{def types de courbes}
        A separating curve $c$ on a connected surface $\Sigma$ is said to be:
    \begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
        \item a \textit{leaf curve} if one of the connected components of $\Sigma\setminus c$ is of complexity $1$ or $1+\delta_{\Sigma}$;
        \item \textit{strongly separating} if none of the two connected components are of complexity $0$;
        \item \textit{weakly separating} if one of the two connected components is of complexity $0$.
    \end{itemize}
    Denote by $\mathcal{C}_{leaf}$ the subset of $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ composed of leaf curves and $\mathcal{C}_{nl}$ the complement, i.e. the \textit{non-leaf curves}. A subsurface of $\Sigma$ of complexity $1$ or $1+\delta_{\Sigma}$ having one leaf curve as its boundary is called a \textit{leaf subsurface}.
<!-- end def-4 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lemma strongly sep curves into decompo}
Any strongly separating curve can be completed into a multicurve which is either a $0$-maximal decomposition or a $1$-maximal decomposition.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let $c$ be a strongly separating curve of $\Sigma$.
    
Assume that $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is even. Let us show that $c$ can be completed into a multicurve which is a $0$-maximal decomposition. Indeed, one component of $\Sigma\setminus c$ has odd complexity, so there are only complexity $1$ surfaces in a $0$-maximal decomposition and the other component of $\Sigma\setminus c$ has even complexity so exactly one complexity $2$ component in a $0$-maximal decomposition. Gluing these two components together, we get a $0$-maximal decomposition of the whole surface.

Now, assume that $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is odd. Let us show that $c$ can be completed into a multicurve which is either a $0$-maximal decomposition or a $1$-maximal decomposition. Indeed, either both components of $\Sigma\setminus c$ have odd complexities and the $0$-maximal decompositions on both sides have only complexity $1$ components, so gluing them together gives a $0$-maximal decomposition. Or both components of $\Sigma\setminus c$ have even complexities so each side has one complexity $2$ component, which makes the total decomposition $1$-maximal because it contains two complexity $2$ surfaces.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{prop D les decompos particulieres avec g+p/3}
Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface without boundary and with $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 3$. Let $D$ be the collection of $0$-maximal decomposition multicurves $\sigma$ of $\Sigma$ verifying
$$\# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{leaf}\right) >\# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{nl}\right)~.$$
Then, any leaf curve of $\Sigma$ can be completed into a multicurve in $D$.
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Fix $g$ to be the genus of $\Sigma$ and $p$ the number of marked points of $\Sigma$. Fix $c$ a leaf curve of $\Sigma$ and fix $S$ the open leaf subsurface with boundary $c$. By definition, the subsurface $S$ has complexity $1$ or $1+\delta_{\Sigma}$. Let us prove the proposition by constructing a multicurve that contains $c$ and that is in $D$.
\medbreak
\textbf{Case 1: $\kappa(\Sigma)\leq 6$.} When $\Sigma$ has complexity at most $6$, we show that any $0$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$ is in $D$. Indeed, any $0$-maximal decomposition $\sigma$ of $\Sigma$ (for instance, one that contains $c$) has a dual graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ which is a tree with ${n_{\Sigma}}$ vertices by Lemma~\ref{lemma structure of k max decopos}. But when $\kappa(\Sigma)\leq 6$,
\begin{align*}
    {n_{\Sigma}}=\frac{\kappa(\Sigma)+1-\delta_{\Sigma}}{2}\leq 3
\end{align*}
so $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is a line of at most three vertices. Thus, all edges of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ are connected to a leaf. This means exactly that $\sigma$ has only leaf curves.

If $\kappa(\Sigma)\leq 4$, then $\{c\}$ gives a $0$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$. When $\kappa(\Sigma)\in\{5,6\}$, then for any leaf curve $c'$ in $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma\setminus S)$, the multicurve $\{c,c'\}$ is a $0$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$.
\medbreak
\textbf{Case 2: $\kappa(\Sigma)> 6$.}\begin{Claim}\label{claim subcase 2a 2b}
    There exists a $0$-maximal decomposition $\sigma$ of $\Sigma$ that contains $c$ and verifies $$\# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{leaf}\right) \geq 2{n_{\Sigma}}/3~.$$
\end{Claim}

Given $\sigma$ as in Claim~\ref{claim subcase 2a 2b}, let us conlude the proof of the proposition by showing that $\sigma\in D$. Note that $\#\sigma={n_{\Sigma}} -1$ because the dual graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is a tree with ${n_{\Sigma}}$ vertices by Lemma~\ref{lemma structure of k max decopos} and the curves of $\sigma$ are in one-to-one correspondence with the edges of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$. The number of non-leaf curves of $\sigma$ is
\begin{align*}
    \# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{nl}\right)&= {n_{\Sigma}} -1-\# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{leaf}\right)\\
                                    &\leq {n_{\Sigma}} -1-\frac{2{n_{\Sigma}}}{3}=\frac{{n_{\Sigma}}}{3}-1\\
                                    &< \frac{2{n_{\Sigma}}}{3}\leq \# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{leaf}\right)
\end{align*}
so $\sigma\in D$. Therefore, we have constructed a $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve in $D$ that completes the leaf curve $c$, which proves the proposition. It is left to prove Claim~\ref{claim subcase 2a 2b}.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Claim~\ref{claim subcase 2a 2b}]
\textbf{Subcase 2.a: $\kappa(\Sigma)> 6$ and $\kappa(S)=1$.} We will construct a $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve that contains $c$ and has $g+\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ leaf curves in total. First, fix $S$ as the first component of the decomposition and $c$ as the first leaf curve.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $S$ is a surface of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$, then $\Sigma\setminus S$ has genus $g-1$. Separate $g-1$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$ and get $g-1$ more leaf curves. Then, separate $\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$ and get $\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ additional leaf curves.
    \item If $S$ is a surface of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$, then $\Sigma\setminus S$ has $p-3$ marked points. Separate $\left\lfloor {p/3} \right\rfloor-1$ subsurfaces of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$ and get $\left\lfloor {p/3} \right\rfloor-1$ more leaf curves. Then, separate $g$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$ to get $g$ leaf curves. 
\end{itemize}
In both cases, as illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig l leaf curves}, we get $\ell$ leaf curves with $$\ell=g+\left\lfloor {p/3} \right\rfloor~.$$

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \includesvg[width=0.8\textwidth]{claim118}
    \caption{The $g+\left\lfloor {p/3} \right\rfloor$ leaf curves and the surface $\Sigma'$ that is left.}
    \label{fig l leaf curves}
\end{figure}


Recall that $${n_{\Sigma}} =\frac{3g+p-2-\delta_{\Sigma}}{2}$$
so that $$\ell\geq g+\frac{p-2}{3}\geq \frac{3g+p-2-\delta_{\Sigma}}{3}=\frac{2{n_{\Sigma}}}{3}~.$$
In the middle, there remains a closed subsurface $\Sigma'$ with $p-3\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ marked points and $g+\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ boundary components, the $\ell$ leaf curves. The assumption $\kappa(\Sigma)> 6$ ensures us that $\Sigma'$ is neither a pair of pants nor an annulus, so in particular the $\ell$ leaf curves constructed above are pairwise distinct. In summary, $\Sigma'$ has type $\Sigma_{0,p',b'}$ with $$p'+b'=g+p-2\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor\geq 4~.$$
Note that $\kappa(\Sigma')$ and $\kappa(\Sigma)$ have the same parity.
Construct a $0$-maximal decomposition $\sigma'$ of $\Sigma'$ as in Example~\ref{exemple de decompo pour sphere n trous}. The vertices of $\Gamma_{\sigma'}$ all have complexity~$1$, except for one complexity $2$ vertex when $\kappa(\Sigma')$ is even. Let $\sigma$ be the multicurve composed of the $\ell$ leaf curves constructed above and of the curves of $\sigma'$. Then, $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is the tree $\Gamma_{\sigma'}$ to which we have added $\ell$ additional leaves of complexity $1$. By Remark~\ref{rem si decompo est un arbre à petites composantes alors cest maximal}, $\sigma$ is a $0$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$.

\medbreak
\textbf{Subcase 2.b: $\kappa(\Sigma)> 6$ and $\kappa(S)=2$.} Note that in this case, $\delta_{\Sigma}=1$ by definition of leaf curves, so $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is necessarily even. We will construct a $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve that contains $c$ and has $g+\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$ leaf curves in total. First, fix $S$ as the first component of the decomposition and $c$ as the first curve.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $S$ is a surface of type $\Sigma_{1,1,1}$, then $\Sigma\setminus S$ has genus $g-1$ and $p-1$ marked points. Separate $g-1$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$ and get $g-1$ more leaf curves. Then, separate $\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$ and get $\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$ additional leaf curves.
    \item If $S$ is a surface of type $\Sigma_{0,4,1}$, then $\Sigma\setminus S$ has $p-4$ marked points and genus $g$. Separate $\left\lfloor {(p-1)/3} \right\rfloor-1$ subsurfaces of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$ and get $\left\lfloor {(p-1)/3} \right\rfloor-1$ more leaf curves. Then, separate $g$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$ to get $g$ additional leaf curves. 
\end{itemize}
In both cases, we get $\ell=g+\left\lfloor {(p-1)/3} \right\rfloor$ leaf curves. Again, the inequality $\ell\geq 2{n_{\Sigma}}/3$ holds because
\begin{align*}
    \ell\geq g+\frac{p-1-2}{3}= g+\frac{p-2-\delta_{\Sigma}}{3}=\frac{2{n_{\Sigma}}}{3}~.
\end{align*}
There remains a surface $\Sigma'$ with $$p'=p-1-3\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$$ marked points and $$b'=g+\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$$ boundary components (the $\ell$ constructed leaf curves). Here, $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is even and at least $7$ so $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 8$. This ensures that $\Sigma'$ is neither a pair of pants nor an annulus. In summary, $\Sigma'$ has type $\Sigma_{0,p',b'}$ with $$p'+b'=g+(p-1)-2\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor\geq 4~.$$
Note that $\kappa(\Sigma)$ being even means that $g+p$ is odd, so $\kappa(\Sigma')$ is odd.
Construct a $0$-maximal decomposition $\sigma'$ of $\Sigma'$ as in Example~\ref{exemple de decompo pour sphere n trous}. The vertices of $\Gamma_{\sigma'}$ all have complexity~$1$. Let $\sigma$ be the multicurve composed of the $\ell$ leaf curves constructed above and of the curves of $\sigma'$. Then, $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is the tree $\Gamma_{\sigma'}$ with $\ell-1$ additional leaves of complexity $1$ and one additional leaf of complexity $2$ (corresponding to $S$). By Remark~\ref{rem si decompo est un arbre à petites composantes alors cest maximal}, $\sigma$ is a $0$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$.\qedhere 
\end{proof}\qedhere
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- claim-1: body_md -->
\label{claim subcase 2a 2b}
    There exists a $0$-maximal decomposition $\sigma$ of $\Sigma$ that contains $c$ and verifies $$\# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{leaf}\right) \geq 2{n_{\Sigma}}/3~.$$
<!-- end claim-1 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
\textbf{Subcase 2.a: $\kappa(\Sigma)> 6$ and $\kappa(S)=1$.} We will construct a $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve that contains $c$ and has $g+\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ leaf curves in total. First, fix $S$ as the first component of the decomposition and $c$ as the first leaf curve.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $S$ is a surface of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$, then $\Sigma\setminus S$ has genus $g-1$. Separate $g-1$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$ and get $g-1$ more leaf curves. Then, separate $\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$ and get $\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ additional leaf curves.
    \item If $S$ is a surface of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$, then $\Sigma\setminus S$ has $p-3$ marked points. Separate $\left\lfloor {p/3} \right\rfloor-1$ subsurfaces of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$ and get $\left\lfloor {p/3} \right\rfloor-1$ more leaf curves. Then, separate $g$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$ to get $g$ leaf curves. 
\end{itemize}
In both cases, as illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig l leaf curves}, we get $\ell$ leaf curves with $$\ell=g+\left\lfloor {p/3} \right\rfloor~.$$

\begin{figure}[ht]
    \centering
    \includesvg[width=0.8\textwidth]{claim118}
    \caption{The $g+\left\lfloor {p/3} \right\rfloor$ leaf curves and the surface $\Sigma'$ that is left.}
    \label{fig l leaf curves}
\end{figure}


Recall that $${n_{\Sigma}} =\frac{3g+p-2-\delta_{\Sigma}}{2}$$
so that $$\ell\geq g+\frac{p-2}{3}\geq \frac{3g+p-2-\delta_{\Sigma}}{3}=\frac{2{n_{\Sigma}}}{3}~.$$
In the middle, there remains a closed subsurface $\Sigma'$ with $p-3\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ marked points and $g+\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor$ boundary components, the $\ell$ leaf curves. The assumption $\kappa(\Sigma)> 6$ ensures us that $\Sigma'$ is neither a pair of pants nor an annulus, so in particular the $\ell$ leaf curves constructed above are pairwise distinct. In summary, $\Sigma'$ has type $\Sigma_{0,p',b'}$ with $$p'+b'=g+p-2\left\lfloor p/3\right\rfloor\geq 4~.$$
Note that $\kappa(\Sigma')$ and $\kappa(\Sigma)$ have the same parity.
Construct a $0$-maximal decomposition $\sigma'$ of $\Sigma'$ as in Example~\ref{exemple de decompo pour sphere n trous}. The vertices of $\Gamma_{\sigma'}$ all have complexity~$1$, except for one complexity $2$ vertex when $\kappa(\Sigma')$ is even. Let $\sigma$ be the multicurve composed of the $\ell$ leaf curves constructed above and of the curves of $\sigma'$. Then, $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is the tree $\Gamma_{\sigma'}$ to which we have added $\ell$ additional leaves of complexity $1$. By Remark~\ref{rem si decompo est un arbre à petites composantes alors cest maximal}, $\sigma$ is a $0$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$.

\medbreak
\textbf{Subcase 2.b: $\kappa(\Sigma)> 6$ and $\kappa(S)=2$.} Note that in this case, $\delta_{\Sigma}=1$ by definition of leaf curves, so $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is necessarily even. We will construct a $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve that contains $c$ and has $g+\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$ leaf curves in total. First, fix $S$ as the first component of the decomposition and $c$ as the first curve.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $S$ is a surface of type $\Sigma_{1,1,1}$, then $\Sigma\setminus S$ has genus $g-1$ and $p-1$ marked points. Separate $g-1$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$ and get $g-1$ more leaf curves. Then, separate $\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$ and get $\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$ additional leaf curves.
    \item If $S$ is a surface of type $\Sigma_{0,4,1}$, then $\Sigma\setminus S$ has $p-4$ marked points and genus $g$. Separate $\left\lfloor {(p-1)/3} \right\rfloor-1$ subsurfaces of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$ and get $\left\lfloor {(p-1)/3} \right\rfloor-1$ more leaf curves. Then, separate $g$ surfaces of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$ to get $g$ additional leaf curves. 
\end{itemize}
In both cases, we get $\ell=g+\left\lfloor {(p-1)/3} \right\rfloor$ leaf curves. Again, the inequality $\ell\geq 2{n_{\Sigma}}/3$ holds because
\begin{align*}
    \ell\geq g+\frac{p-1-2}{3}= g+\frac{p-2-\delta_{\Sigma}}{3}=\frac{2{n_{\Sigma}}}{3}~.
\end{align*}
There remains a surface $\Sigma'$ with $$p'=p-1-3\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$$ marked points and $$b'=g+\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor$$ boundary components (the $\ell$ constructed leaf curves). Here, $\kappa(\Sigma)$ is even and at least $7$ so $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 8$. This ensures that $\Sigma'$ is neither a pair of pants nor an annulus. In summary, $\Sigma'$ has type $\Sigma_{0,p',b'}$ with $$p'+b'=g+(p-1)-2\left\lfloor (p-1)/3\right\rfloor\geq 4~.$$
Note that $\kappa(\Sigma)$ being even means that $g+p$ is odd, so $\kappa(\Sigma')$ is odd.
Construct a $0$-maximal decomposition $\sigma'$ of $\Sigma'$ as in Example~\ref{exemple de decompo pour sphere n trous}. The vertices of $\Gamma_{\sigma'}$ all have complexity~$1$. Let $\sigma$ be the multicurve composed of the $\ell$ leaf curves constructed above and of the curves of $\sigma'$. Then, $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is the tree $\Gamma_{\sigma'}$ with $\ell-1$ additional leaves of complexity $1$ and one additional leaf of complexity $2$ (corresponding to $S$). By Remark~\ref{rem si decompo est un arbre à petites composantes alors cest maximal}, $\sigma$ is a $0$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$.\qedhere
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- def-5: body_md -->
A \textit{Borel groupoid} over a standard Borel space $X$ is a standard Borel space $\mathcal{G}$ equipped with the following: 
    \begin{itemize}[[noitemsep, nolistsep, label=-]
        \item two measurable maps $s,r : \mathcal{G} \longrightarrow X$ called the \textit{source} and \textit{range} maps;
        \item a measurable map $({\mathfrak g},{\mathfrak h})\longmapsto {\mathfrak g}{\mathfrak h}$ defined on $\mathcal{G}^{(2)}=\{({\mathfrak g},{\mathfrak h})\in\mathcal{G} ~|~ r({\mathfrak h})=s({\mathfrak g})\}$
     and called the \textit{composition} map;
        \item a measurable map 
        $$\begin{array}{rcl}
            X & \longrightarrow & \mathcal{G} \\
            x & \longmapsto & {\mathfrak e}_x
        \end{array}$$
        such that for any $x\in X$ and any ${\mathfrak g},{\mathfrak h}\in\mathcal{G}$ with $s({\mathfrak g})=x=r({\mathfrak h})$,
        \begin{align*}
            s({\mathfrak e}_x)=r({\mathfrak e}_x)=x,~{\mathfrak g}{\mathfrak e}_x={\mathfrak g}\text{ and }{\mathfrak e}_x{\mathfrak h}={\mathfrak h}
        \end{align*}
       called the \textit{unit} map;
        \item a measurable map 
        $$\begin{array}{rcl}
            \mathcal{G} & \longrightarrow & \mathcal{G} \\
            {\mathfrak g} & \longmapsto & {\mathfrak g}^{-1}
        \end{array}$$
        such that for any ${\mathfrak g}\in\mathcal{G}$, 
        $$s({\mathfrak g}^{-1})=r({\mathfrak g}),~r({\mathfrak g}^{-1})=s({\mathfrak g}),~{\mathfrak g}{\mathfrak g}^{-1}={\mathfrak e}_{r({\mathfrak g})}\text{ and } {\mathfrak g}^{-1}{\mathfrak g}={\mathfrak e}_{s({\mathfrak g})}$$
        called the \textit{inverse} map.
    \end{itemize}
    The space $X$ is called the \textit{base space} of the groupoid $\mathcal{G}$. The groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ is called \textit{discrete} when $s^{-1}(\{x\})$ is countable for every $x\in X$.

    A \textit{subgroupoid} of $\mathcal{G}$ is a Borel subset $\mathcal{H}\subseteq \mathcal{G}$ that is stable by composition and inverse, and that contains $\mathfrak e_x$ for all $x\in X$. The subgroupoid \textit{generated} by two subgroupoids $\mathcal{H}$ and $\mathcal{H}'$ of $\mathcal{G}$ is the smallest subgroupoid of $\mathcal{G}$ that contains $\mathcal{H}$ and $\mathcal{H}'$. It is denoted $\langle {\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}'} \rangle$.
<!-- end def-5 -->
<!-- def-6: body_md -->
The \textit{restriction} of a Borel groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ to a Borel subset $A\subseteq X$ is a Borel groupoid over the space $A$ defined by $\mathcal{G}|_A=\{g\in\mathcal{G}~|~s(g),r(g)\in A\}~.$
<!-- end def-6 -->
<!-- def-7: body_md -->
A \textit{bisection} of a Borel groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ is a measurable subset of $\mathcal{G}$ such that $s|_B$ and $r|_B$ are Borel isomorphisms onto their images $s(B)$ and $r(B)$.
<!-- end def-7 -->
<!-- def-8: body_md -->
A \textit{measured groupoid} is a discrete Borel groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ over a standard measured space $(X,\mu)$ such that for any bisection $B\subseteq \mathcal{G}$, $\mu(s(B))=\mu(r(B))$.
<!-- end def-8 -->
<!-- def-9: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard measured space $X$. Let $G$ be a countable group. A \textit{cocycle} is a measurable map $\rho : \mathcal{G} \longrightarrow G$ such that 
    $$\forall ({\mathfrak g},{\mathfrak h})\in\mathcal{G}^{(2)},~\rho({\mathfrak g}{\mathfrak h})=\rho({\mathfrak g})\rho({\mathfrak h})~.$$
    The \textit{kernel} of $\rho$ is $\ker \rho =\{{\mathfrak g}\in\mathcal{G}~|~\rho({\mathfrak g})=1_{G}\}$ and we say that $\rho$ has \textit{trivial kernel} if $\ker\rho=\{{\mathfrak e}_x\}_{x\in X}$.
<!-- end def-9 -->
<!-- def-10: body_md -->
A measured groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ over $(X,\mu)$ is of \textit{infinite type} if for any Borel subset $A\subseteq X$ of positive measure and for almost every $x\in A$, the set $\mathcal{G}|_A\cap s^{-1}(\{x\})$ is infinite.
<!-- end def-10 -->
<!-- rem-4: body_md -->
\label{rem trivial kernel infinite-type groupoid to infinite group}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid and let $G$ be a group. Let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow G$ be a cocycle with trivial kernel. If $\mathcal{G}$ is of infinite type, then the group $G$ is infinite.
<!-- end rem-4 -->
<!-- def-11: body_md -->
A cocycle $\rho :\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow G$ is of \textit{action type} when it has trivial kernel and when for any infinite subgroup $H$ of $G$, the groupoid $\rho^{-1}(H)$ is of infinite type.
<!-- end def-11 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{cocycles from pmp actions are action-type}
    Let $G$ be a countable group admitting a measure-preserving action on a standard measured space $(X,\mu)$ with $\mu(X)$ finite. Then, the groupoid $(G\ltimes X)|_A$ is of infinite type and the cocycle $(G\ltimes X)|_A \longrightarrow G$ is of action type for every Borel subset $A\subseteq X$ of positive measure.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- def-12: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard measured space $X$ and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow G$ be a cocycle into a countable group $G$. Assume that $G$ has a measure-preserving action on a standard measured space $Y$. A measurable map $\varphi :X\longrightarrow Y$ is $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$\textit{-equivariant} if there exists a conull Borel subset $X^*$ of $X$ such that $\forall g\in\mathcal{G}|_{X^*}$, 
    $$\varphi(r(g))=\rho(g)\varphi(s(g))~.$$
<!-- end def-12 -->
<!-- def-13: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard measured space $X$, $\mathcal{H}$ a subgroupoid of $\mathcal{G}$ and $B$ a bisection of $\mathcal{G}$. Then, $\mathcal{H}$ is $B$\textit{-invariant} if there exists a conull Borel subset $X^*\subseteq X$ such that for any ${\mathfrak b},{\mathfrak b}'\in B\cap \mathcal{G}|_{X^*}$ and ${\mathfrak h}\in\mathcal{G}|_{X^*}$ such that ${\mathfrak b}'{\mathfrak h}{\mathfrak b}^{-1}$ is well defined, it holds that 
    $${\mathfrak h}\in\mathcal{H}\iff {\mathfrak b}'{\mathfrak h}{\mathfrak b}^{-1}\in\mathcal{H}~.$$
<!-- end def-13 -->
<!-- def-14: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{H},\mathcal{A}$ be subgroupoids of a measured groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ over a space $X$.
    Then, $\mathcal{H}$ \textit{normalises} $\mathcal{A}$ if there exist bisections $(B_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ such that $\mathcal{H}=\bigcup_n B_n$ and $\mathcal{A}$ is $B_n$-invariant for every $n$.
<!-- end def-14 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lemma if groups normalise then groupoids normalise}
    Let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow G$ be a cocycle from a measured groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ to a countable group $G$. If a subgroup $H$ of $G$ normalises another subgroup $A$ then $\rho^{-1}(H)$ normalises $\rho^{-1}(A)$.\qed
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lemma triv kernel cocycle amenable group amenable groupoid}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid and let $G$ a countable group. Let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow G$ be a cocycle with trivial kernel. 
    
    If $G$ is amenable, then $\mathcal{G}$ is amenable.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- def-15: body_md -->
A groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ over a space $X$ is \textit{everywhere nonamenable} if for every Borel subset $A\subseteq X$ of positive measure, $\mathcal{G}|_A$ is not amenable.
<!-- end def-15 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lemme action-type groupe non moyennable donne groupoide non moyennable}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$ and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow G$ be an action-type cocycle into a countable group $ G$. 
    
    If $ G$ contains a nonabelian free group, then $\mathcal{G}$ is everywhere nonamenable.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- def-16: body_md -->
Let $G,H$ be two countable groups. We say that $H$ \textit{measurably embeds} into $G$ if there exists a standard measured space $(\Omega,m)$ with a measure-preserving action of $G\times H$ such that
    \begin{itemize}[noitemsep, nolistsep]
        \item the action $G\curvearrowright \Omega$ is free and admits a Borel fundamental domain of finite positive measure,
        \item the action $H\curvearrowright \Omega$ is free and admits a Borel fundamental domain.
    \end{itemize}
    Such a space $\Omega$ is called a \textit{measurable embedding coupling} of $H$ into $G$. 
    
    If additionally the action $H\curvearrowright \Omega$ admits a Borel fundamental domain of finite measure, then we say that $H$ and $G$ are \textit{measure equivalent} and $\Omega$ is a \textit{measure equivalence coupling} of $H$ and~$G$.
<!-- end def-16 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lemma measurable embedding provides groupoid and cocycles}
    Let $G,H$ be two countable groups. Let $(\Omega,m)$ be a measurable embedding coupling of $H$ into $G$.
    
    Then, there exist
    \begin{itemize}[noitemsep, nolistsep]
        \item a Borel subset $X$ of $\Omega$ of finite positive measure such that $\left(G\times H\right) X$ is conull in $\Omega$,
        \item a measured groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ with base space $X$, an action-type cocycle $\omega : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow H$ and a trivial-kernel cocycle $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow G$.
    \end{itemize}
    Up to replacing $m$ by $m/m(X)$, one can consider $X$ to be a standard probability space.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
By \cite[Lemma~2.27]{kida_introduction_2009}, it is possible to choose Borel fundamental domains $D_G$ for $G$ and $D_H$ for $H$ such that $$m\left(\Omega\setminus\left(G\times H\right) X\right)=0~, $$where $X=D_G\cap D_H$. In particular, this implies that $X$ has positive measure. As $D_G$ has finite measure, $X$ also has finite measure. Note that \cite[Lemma~2.27]{kida_introduction_2009} is stated in the case of a measure equivalence coupling, but the proof is exactly the same for a measurable embedding coupling.

The actions of $G$ and of $H$ on $\Omega$ commute, so $G$ acts on $D_H$ and $H$ acts on $D_G$ via measure-preserving actions we denote by $\cdot$ to differentiate them from the actions on $\Omega$. Expliciting those actions provides us with two cocycles. For $d\in D_{ H}$ and $ g\in G$, define $ g\cdot d$ as being the unique representative in $D_{ H}$ of the $ H$-orbit of $ g d$, i.e.\
$$\{ g\cdot d\}= D_{ H}\cap  H( g d)~.$$
Similarly, for $h\in H$ and $d\in D_G$, $$\{ h\cdot d\}= D_{ G}\cap  G( h d)~.$$
Taking the exact element of $ H$ that brings $ g d$ to $D_{ H}$ defines the following cocycle:
$$\omega : \begin{array}{ccl}
     G\ltimes D_{ H} & \longrightarrow &  H \\
     ( g, d)&\longmapsto & \text{the unique } h\in H \text{ such that }  h g d= g\cdot d~.
\end{array}$$
Symmetrically, the action $ H\stackrel{\textstyle \cdot}{\curvearrowright}D_{ G}$ gives the cocycle 
$$\rho : \begin{array}{ccl}
     H\ltimes D_{ G} & \longrightarrow &  G \\
     ( h, d)&\longmapsto & \text{the unique } g\in G \text{ such that }g h d =h\cdot d~.
\end{array}$$
Now, restricting to $X=D_G\cap D_H$, there is a groupoid isomorphism
$$\begin{array}{ccc}
     \big(( G\times H)\ltimes \Omega\big)|_X &\simeq &( G\ltimes D_{ H})|_X\\
     \left(( g, h),x\right)& \longmapsto & ( g,x)
\end{array}$$
because for fixed $ g\in G$ and $x\in X$, the only possible $ h\in H$ such that $( g, h)x\in X$ is $\omega( g,x)$. The cocycle $\omega$ becomes 
$$\omega : \begin{array}{ccl}
     \big(( G\times H)\ltimes \Omega\big)|_X &\longrightarrow & H\\
     \left(( g, h),x\right)& \longmapsto &  h~.
\end{array}$$
But we also have the groupoid isomorphism
$$\begin{array}{ccc}
     \big(( G\times H)\ltimes \Omega\big)|_X &\simeq &( H\ltimes D_{ G})|_X\\
     \left(( g, h),x\right)& \longmapsto & ( h,x)
\end{array}$$
which now gives the cocycle $\omega$ as the natural cocycle from the action of $H$ on $D_G$
$$\omega : \begin{array}{ccl}
     ( H\ltimes D_{ G})|_X &\longrightarrow & H\\
     ( h,x)& \longmapsto &  h~.
\end{array}$$
Symmetrically, $\rho : ( G\ltimes D_{ H})|_X\longrightarrow G$.
To sum up, define the common groupoid
$$\mathcal{G} = (H\ltimes D_{G})|_X \simeq (G\ltimes D_{H})|_X~ .$$
Via this identification, the cocycles defined above arise from group actions
$$\omega : (H\ltimes D_{G})|_X \longrightarrow H~ ,$$
$$\rho : (G\ltimes D_{H})|_X \longrightarrow G~.$$
Thus, since $D_G$ has finite measure, the cocycle $\omega$ is of action type by Lemma~\ref{cocycles from pmp actions are action-type}.
The difference with measure equivalence lies in the fact that there is no reason for $\rho $ to be of action type because it comes from an action on a space of infinite measure. The cocycle $\rho $ still has trivial kernel because it comes from a group action.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- rem-5: body_md -->
\label{alg embedding is meas embedding}
    For countable groups $G$ and $H$, an injective homomorphism $f : H\longrightarrow G$ is a special case of measurable embedding. Indeed, $G=\Omega$ is a measurable embedding coupling of $H$ into $G$, where $G$ acts by left translations and $f(H)$ by right translations. This coupling corresponds to $X$ being one single point of $G$. The groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ given by Lemma~\ref{lemma measurable embedding provides groupoid and cocycles} is then isomorphic to the group $H$, the cocycle $\omega$ is the identity on $H$ and the cocycle $\rho $ is the embedding~$f$.
<!-- end rem-5 -->
<!-- rem-6: body_md -->
\label{rem amen is preserved by meas emb} Take a measurable embedding from a countable group $H$ to a countable group $G$, apply Lemma~\ref{lemma measurable embedding provides groupoid and cocycles} and adopt the same notations. If $G$ is amenable, the trivial-kernel cocycle $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow G$ forces the groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ to be amenable, by Lemma~\ref{lemma triv kernel cocycle amenable group amenable groupoid}. The action-type cocycle $\omega:\mathcal{G} \longrightarrow H$ has the following additional property: if $H$ is nonamenable, then $\mathcal{G}$ is everywhere nonamenable, by \cite[Lemma~4.12]{escalier_graph_2026}. In other words, $\omega$ is \textit{action-like} in the sense of \cite[Definition~4.9]{escalier_graph_2026}. In conclusion, if $H$ measurably embeds into $G$ and $G$ is amenable, then $H$ is too.
<!-- end rem-6 -->
<!-- rem-7: body_md -->
\label{remark finite indices in me couplings}
Let $\Omega$ be a measurable embedding coupling of $H$ into $G$ with Borel fundamental domains $D_H$ and $D_G$. Let $H^0$ (resp. $G^0$) be a finite-index subgroup of $H$ (resp. $G$) with respective finite systems of representatives $R_H$ and $R_G$. Define the Borel subsets 
$$D_G^0=R_GD_G\text{ and }D_H^0=R_HD_H~.$$
Then, $D_G^0$ (resp. $D_H^0$) is a fundamental domain for the induced action of $G^0$ (resp. $H^0$) on $\Omega$. Moreover, $D_G^0$ has finite positive measure because it is the union of a finite number of translates of $D_G$. Thus, $\Omega$ is also a measurable embedding coupling of $H^0$ into $G^0$. 

Moreover, $D_H^0 = \bigsqcup_{h\in R_H} h D_H$ so $D_H^0$ has finite measure if and only if $D_H$ has finite measure. Therefore, $\Omega$ is a measure equivalence coupling of $H$ and $G$ if and only if it is a measure equivalence coupling of $H^0$ and $G^0$.
<!-- end rem-7 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{transitivité du plongement ME} Let $G_1,G_2$ and $H$ be countable groups. 

If $G_1$ measurably embeds into $H$ and $H$ into $G_2$, then $G_1$ measurably embeds into $G_2$. If additionally every measurable embedding from $G_1$ to $G_2$ is a measure equivalence, then $H$ is measure equivalent to $G_1$ and $G_2$.
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
For $i\in\{1,2\}$, fix $G_i\times H\curvearrowright\Omega_i$ a measurable embedding coupling. Denote the corresponding fundamental domains $X_i$ for $H$ and $Y_i$ for $G_i$, i.e.\
\begin{align*}
    \Omega_i&\simeq  H\times X_i\\
          &\simeq G_i\times Y_i~.
\end{align*} 
The Borel subsets $Y_2$ and $X_1$ have finite measure.

The composed coupling is defined by 
$$\Omega =~^{\textstyle\Omega_1\times\Omega_2}\Big/ _{\textstyle  H}$$
where $H$ acts diagonally on $\Omega_1\times\Omega_2$. Using the fact that $\Omega_1/H\simeq X_1$, we identify
$$\Omega\simeq X_1\times\Omega_2~.$$
This exhibits an action of $G_2$ on $\Omega$ by acting on the second coordinate. A Borel fundamental domain for this action is therefore $X_1\times Y_2$, which has finite measure. Symmetrically, the indentification 
$$\Omega\simeq \Omega_1\times X_2 $$
gives an action of $G_1$ on $\Omega$ with Borel fundamental domain $Y_1\times X_2$. The actions of $G_1$ and $G_2$ on $\Omega$ commute and are free, see e.g.\ \cite[Appendix~A.1.3]{bader_integrable_2013} for the explicit formulas. Hence $\Omega$ is indeed a measurable embedding coupling from $G_1$ to $G_2$.

Now, assume in addition that every measurable embedding coupling from $G_1$ into $G_2$ is a measure equivalence. This forces the Borel subset $Y_1\times X_2$ to also have finite measure in $\Omega_1\times X_2$. If either $Y_1$ or $X_2$ had infinite measure, then $Y_1\times X_2$ also would have infinite measure. Thus, $Y_1$ and $X_2$ both have finite measure. Therefore, $H$ is measure equivalent to $G_1$ via the coupling $\Omega_1$ and to $G_2$ via $\Omega_2$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{prop adams maximal map}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be an infinite-type amenable measured groupoid on a standard probability space $X$. Let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow F_2$ be a cocycle with trivial kernel. 
    
    Then, there exists a measurable and $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$-equivariant map $\varphi_{\text{max}} : X\longrightarrow\mathrm{Prob}(\partial F_2)$ that verifies the following.
    \begin{enumerate}[$(1)$]
        \item \label{1support}\textbf{Support.} There exists a conull Borel subset $X^*$ of $X$ such that $\varphi_{\text{max}}|_{X^*}$ takes values in $\mathrm{Prob}_{\leq 2}(\partial F_2)$.
        \item \label{2maximality}\textbf{Maximality.} If $\varphi: X \longrightarrow \mathrm{Prob}( \partial F_2)$ is a measurable and $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$-equivariant map, then for almost every $x\in X$, $\mathrm{Supp}~(\varphi(x))\subseteq \mathrm{Supp}~ (\varphi_{\text{max}}(x))$.
        \item \label{3uniqueness}\textbf{Uniqueness.} If there exists another map $\varphi_{\text{max}}'$ that verifies Points~\ref{1support} and \ref{2maximality}, then for almost every $x\in X$, $\varphi_{\text{max}}(x)=\varphi_{\text{max}}'(x)$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
The groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ being amenable, by \cite[Proposition~4.14]{kida_introduction_2009}, there exists a measurable and $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$-equivariant map ${X\longrightarrow \mathrm{Prob}(\partial F_2)}$. In \cite[Proposition~5.1]{adams_indecomposability_1994}, Adams states that any measurable $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$-equivariant map ${X\longrightarrow \mathrm{Prob}(\partial F_2)}$ takes values in $\mathrm{Prob}_{\leq 2}(\partial F_2)$ almost everywhere. This map is therefore almost everywhere valued in $\mathrm{Prob}_{\leq 2}(\partial F_2)$.
    Then, this existence allows us to apply \cite[Lemma~3.2]{adams_indecomposability_1994} which gives the existence of a map ${\varphi_{\text{max}} : X\longrightarrow \mathrm{Prob}_{\leq 2}(\partial F_2)}$ that verifies Points~\ref{1support} and \ref{2maximality}. By \cite[Lemma~3.3]{adams_indecomposability_1994}, Point~\ref{3uniqueness} holds as well.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- rem-8: body_md -->
Strictly speaking, Adams states the previous results only for action-type cocycles because he works with probability measure-preserving actions. Fortunately, the proof is exactly the same for cocycles with trivial kernel. For instance, we refer to the proof of \cite[Lemma~12.2]{guirardel_measure_2025} where Adams arguments are rewritten for trivial-kernel cocycles.
<!-- end rem-8 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lemma A,B in F2 nonamenable}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid on a standard probability space $X$. Let ${F_2=\langle {a,b} \rangle}$ be a nonabelian free group and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow F_2$ be a cocycle with trivial kernel. Let $\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}$ be infinite-type subgroupoids of $\mathcal{G}$ such that $\rho(\mathcal{A})\subseteq \langle {a} \rangle$ and $\rho(\mathcal{B})\subseteq \langle {b} \rangle$.
    
    Then, for every Borel subset $U$ of $X$ of positive measure, the subgroupoid $\langle {\mathcal{A}|_U,\mathcal{B}|_U} \rangle$ is everywhere nonamenable.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Assume by contradiction that there exists a positive measure Borel subset $Y$ of $U$ such that the subgroupoid $$\mathcal{H}=\langle {\mathcal{A}|_U,\mathcal{B}|_U} \rangle|_Y$$ is amenable. Then, by Proposition~\ref{prop adams maximal map}, there exists a measurable and $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$-equivariant map $$\varphi_{\mathcal{H}} : Y\longrightarrow \mathrm{Prob}(\partial F_2)~.$$


Denote by $\zeta_a,\eta_a$ (resp. $\zeta_b,\eta_b$) the two fixed ends of $a$ (resp. $b$) in $\partial F_2$. Denote by $\delta_{\zeta}$ the Dirac measure at an end $\zeta\in\partial F_2$.
The constant map $$\varphi_a : Y\longrightarrow \left\{\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\zeta_a}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\eta_a}\right\}$$
is measurable and $(\mathcal{A}|_Y,\rho)$-equivariant. Similarly, the constant map 
$$\varphi_b : Y\longrightarrow \left\{\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\zeta_b}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\eta_b}\right\}$$ is measurable and $(\mathcal{B}|_Y,\rho)$-equivariant map.

The groupoid $\mathcal{A}|_Y$ is amenable because it is sent into the amenable group $\langle {a} \rangle$ by a cocycle with trivial kernel, by Lemma~\ref{lemma triv kernel cocycle amenable group amenable groupoid}. Proposition~\ref{prop adams maximal map} applied to the groupoid $\mathcal{A}|_Y$ yields the existence of an almost everywhere unique measurable and $(\mathcal{A}|_Y,\rho)$-equivariant map $Y\longrightarrow \mathrm{Prob}_{\leq 2}(\partial F_2)$ that is maximal amongst all measurable $(\mathcal{A}|_Y,\rho)$-equivariant maps $Y\longrightarrow\mathrm{Prob}(\partial F_2)$. Now, notice that the constant map $\varphi_a$ is necessarily equal almost everywhere to this unique maximal map because it has support of cardinality exactly $2$.
Similarly, by Proposition~\ref{prop adams maximal map} applied to $\mathcal{B}|_Y$, the constant map 
$$\varphi_b : Y\longrightarrow \left\{\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\zeta_b}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\eta_b}\right\}$$ is the (almost everywhere) unique maximal measurable $(\mathcal{B}|_Y,\rho)$-equivariant map amongst all measurable $(\mathcal{B}|_Y,\rho)$-equivariant maps $Y\longrightarrow \mathrm{Prob}(\partial F_2)$.

Note that $\mathcal{A}|_Y,\mathcal{B}|_Y\subseteq \mathcal{H}$ so the map $\varphi_{\mathcal{H}}$ is in particular both $(\mathcal{A}|_Y,\rho)$-equivariant and $(\mathcal{B}|_Y,\rho)$-equivariant. By maximality of $\varphi_a$ amongst all measurable and $(\mathcal{A}|_Y,\rho)$-equivariant maps from $Y$ to $\mathrm{Prob}(\partial F_2)$, for almost every $y\in Y$, $$\mathrm{Supp}~(\varphi_{\mathcal{H}}(y))\subseteq\mathrm{Supp}~ (\varphi_a(y))=\{\zeta_a,\eta_a\}~.$$
Similarly, for almost every $y\in Y$, $$\mathrm{Supp}~(\varphi_{\mathcal{H}}(y))\subseteq \{\zeta_b,\eta_b\}~.$$
But $a$ and $b$ generate a free group so $\{\zeta_a,\eta_a\}\cap \{\zeta_b,\eta_b\}$ is empty. Therefore, $\mathcal{H}$ cannot be amenable. In conclusion, $\langle {\mathcal{A}|_U,\mathcal{B}|_U} \rangle$ is everywhere nonamenable.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lemma free product into direct product}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$. Let $G$ and $K$ be infinite countable groups and let $A$ be an infinite cyclic group. Assume that there is an action-type cocycle $$\omega:\mathcal{G} \longrightarrow K\ast A$$ and a cocycle $$\rho:\mathcal{G} \longrightarrow G$$ with trivial kernel.
    Let $\mathcal{K}, \mathcal{A}$ be infinite-type subgroupoids of $\mathcal{G}$ such that 
    \begin{align*}
        \omega(\mathcal{K})\subseteq K~\text{and } \omega(\mathcal{A}) \subseteq A~.
    \end{align*}
    Then, $\rho(\mathcal{A})$ cannot be contained in the centre of $G$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Assume by contradiction that $\rho(\mathcal{A})\subseteq Z(G)$, where $Z(G)$ is the centre of $G$. For $k\in K$ and $g\in G$, 
    $$B_{g,k}=\{\mathfrak k\in\mathcal{K}~|~ \rho(\mathfrak k)=g,~\omega(\mathfrak k)=k\}$$
    is a bisection of $\mathcal{G}$ because $\omega$ and $\rho$ have trivial kernel. Note that $$\mathcal{K}=\bigcup_{\substack{g\in G\\ k\in K}}B_{g,k}~.$$
From now on, fix nontrivial $k\in K$ and $g\in G$ such that the bisection $B_{g,k}$, which we will now simply denote by $B$, has positive measure. Name $U$ the source of $B$ and $V$ its range, so that $U,V$ are Borel subsets of $X$ of positive measure. Now, define the following subgroupoid of $\mathcal{G}|_V$
    $$\mathcal{H}=\langle {\mathcal{A}|_V,B\mathcal{A}|_U B^{-1}} \rangle~.$$
    We will show that $\mathcal{H}$ is both amenable and nonamenable which gives a contradiction. 

    On one hand, we show that $\mathcal{H}$ is amenable. Firstly, $\rho(\mathcal{A}|_V)\subseteq Z(G)$ by assumption. Secondly, fix $\mathfrak a\in\mathcal{A}|_U$ and $\mathfrak b,\mathfrak b'\in B$ such that $\mathfrak{bab'}^{-1}$ is well defined. Then,
    \begin{align*}
        \rho(\mathfrak{bab'}^{-1})&=\rho(\mathfrak b)\rho(\mathfrak a)\rho(\mathfrak b')^{-1}\\
                            &=g\rho(\mathfrak a)g^{-1} = \rho(\mathfrak a)\in Z(G)
    \end{align*}
    so $\rho(B\mathcal{A}|_U B^{-1})\subseteq Z(G)$. Thus $\rho(\mathcal{H})\subseteq Z(G)$. The cocycle $\rho$ having trivial kernel, this forces $\mathcal{H}$ to be amenable by Lemma~\ref{lemma triv kernel cocycle amenable group amenable groupoid}.

    On the other hand, let us show that $\mathcal{H}$ is not amenable. Let $A=\langle {a} \rangle$. Note that $\omega(\mathcal{A}|_V)\subseteq \langle {a} \rangle$ and
    $$\omega(B\mathcal{A}|_U B^{-1})\subseteq k\omega(\mathcal{A}|_U)k^{-1} \subseteq \langle {kak^{-1}} \rangle~.$$
    Using that $a$ and $kak^{-1}$ generate a free group, apply Lemma~\ref{lemma A,B in F2 nonamenable} to find that $\mathcal{H}$ is indeed nonamenable.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- def-17: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$. Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ be a cocycle.
    
    We say that $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$ \textit{stabilises} a multicurve $\sigma$ if ${\rho(\mathcal{G}|_{X^*})\subseteq \mathrm{Stab}(\sigma) }$ for some conull Borel subset $X^*$ of $X$. 
    We say that $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$ \textit{stabilises} an open subsurface $Q$ if $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$ stabilises the multicurve $\partial Q$. In other words, $Q$ is a component of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma$ for some multicurve $\sigma$ that $\mathcal{H}$ stabilises.
<!-- end def-17 -->
<!-- def-18: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$. Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ be a cocycle. A set $\mathcal{C}$ of curves on $\Sigma$ is the \textit{canonical reduction set} of $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$ if 
     \begin{itemize}[nolistsep, label=-]
        \item $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$ stabilises all the curves in $\mathcal{C}$;
        \item for every Borel subset $U$ of $X$ of positive measure, any curve that is stabilised by $(\mathcal{G}|_U,\rho)$ belongs to $\mathcal{C}$.
     \end{itemize}
     The \textit{canonical multicurve} of $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$ is the boundary of the subsurface filled by $\mathcal{C}$.
<!-- end def-18 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{canonical reduction set exists}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$. Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ be a cocycle.
    
    Then, there exists a countable conull Borel partition $X^*=\bigsqcup_n X_n$ of $X$ such that for every $n$, the canonical reduction set and multicurve exist for $(\mathcal{G}|_{X_n},\rho)$.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- rem-9: body_md -->
\label{rem we can always choose U for the canonical multicurve to exist}
In view of the previous lemma, given a finite number of subgroupoids $\mathcal{H}_1,\dots,
$ $\mathcal{H}_k$ of $\mathcal{G}$, there always exists a countable conull Borel partition $X^*=\bigsqcup_n X_n$ such that the canonical multicurves of $(\mathcal{H}_i|_ {X_n},\rho)$ exist for all $1\leq i\leq k$ and all $n\in \mathbb{N}$. Indeed, it suffices to refine the $k$ partitions given by Lemma~\ref{canonical reduction set exists} into a common partition. In this case, we will say that such a partition is \textit{adapted} to the subgroupoids $(\mathcal{H}_i,\rho)$ for all $i$.
<!-- end rem-9 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lemma multicurve is canonical}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$. Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ be a cocycle. Let $\mathcal{A}$ and $\mathcal{H}$ be subgroupoids such that $\mathcal{H}$ normalises $\mathcal{A}$. Assume that the canonical multicurve $\sigma$ of $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ exists. 
    
    Then, $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ stabilises $\sigma$.
If in addition the canonical multicurve $\sigma'$ of $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ exists, then $\sigma$ and $\sigma'$ are disjoint.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- def-19: body_md -->
Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$. Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ be a cocycle with trivial kernel. Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a subgroupoid of $\mathcal{G}$ such that $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ stabilises an open subsurface $Q$ of $\Sigma$. We say that
\begin{itemize}
        \item $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is \textit{trivial} on $Q$ when $\rho(\mathcal{H}|_{X^*})\subseteq\mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q }^*}(\Sigma)$ for some conull Borel subset $X^*\subseteq X$.
    \item $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is \textit{reducible} on $Q$ when $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ stabilises a curve $c\in\mathcal{C}(Q)$ \item $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is \textit{irreducible} on $Q$ if it is nontrivial and not reducible on $Q$. 
\end{itemize}
When $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is irreducible on $Q$, we say that
\begin{itemize} 
    \item $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is \textit{irreducible amenable} on $Q$ when there exists a measurable and $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$-equivariant map $X\longrightarrow \partial_{\leq 2}\mathcal{C}(Q)$,
    \item $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is \textit{irreducible nonamenable} on $Q$ when there does not exist such a map.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end def-19 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{reducible nontrivial implies existence of canonical curve}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$. Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ be a cocycle with trivial kernel. Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a subgroupoid of $\mathcal{G}$ and assume that the canonical multicurve $\sigma$ of $(\mathcal{G},\rho)$ exists. 
    
    If $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ stabilises an open connected subsurface $Q$ and is reducible and nontrivial on $Q$, then $\sigma$ contains a curve of $\mathcal{C}(Q)$.
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Consider the subsurface $\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$ filled by the canonical reduction set of $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$. We have that $\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\cap Q\neq \varnothing$ because $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ fixes some curve of $\mathcal{C}(Q)$. We also have that $Q\nsubseteq \Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$ because $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q$.

    By contradiction, assume that $\mathcal{C}(Q)$ does not contain any curve of $\sigma$. Then, the fact that $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ stabilises $Q$ means that $Q$ is contained in a connected component of $\Sigma \setminus\partial \Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$, because $\sigma=\partial\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$. This contradicts either $\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}\cap Q\neq \varnothing$ or $Q\nsubseteq \Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$.
    
    Therefore, there exists a curve $c\in\mathcal{C}(Q)\cap\partial \Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- rem-10: body_md -->
Note that in \cite[Section~7]{kida_introduction_2009} and \cite[Definition~5.4]{kida_introduction_2009}, Kida defines trivial, irreducible amenable and irreducible nonamenable for a subgroupoid $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ only on components $Q$ of the surface cut along the canonical multicurve $\sigma$ of $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$, assuming it exists. Let us explain that by considering the more general case of a subsurface $Q$ stabilised by $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$, $Q$ is either a component of $\Sigma\setminus\sigma$ like for Kida, or $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is reducible or trivial on $Q$. Assume that $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is nontrivial on an open subsurface $Q$ that it stabilises. \begin{itemize}[noitemsep, nolistsep]
    \item If $\partial Q\nsubseteq \sigma$, then $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is reducible on $Q$ because the subsurface filled by the canonical reduction set of $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ has nontrivial intersection with $Q$.
    \item If $\partial Q\subseteq \sigma$, then $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is reducible on $Q$ if and only if there is another curve of $\sigma$ in $Q$. 
\end{itemize}
So if a subgroupoid $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is irreducible on $Q$, then $Q$ is necessarily a component of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma$. Conversely, if $Q$ is a component of $\Sigma\setminus\sigma$, then $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ can only be trivial or irreducible on $Q$. Moreover, our definitions of irreducible amenable and irreducible nonamenable coincide with Kida's definition, as stated in \cite[Corollary~5.14]{kida_introduction_2009}.
<!-- end rem-10 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{map to product of boundaries of curve graphs of components}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$ and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ be a cocycle with trivial kernel. Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a subgroupoid of $\mathcal{G}$ such that $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ stabilises a multicurve $\sigma$ on $\Sigma$. Let $Q_1,\dots,Q_k$ be the connected components of $\Sigma\setminus\sigma$ on which $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is nontrivial. Assume that there exists a measurable and $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$-equivariant map 
    $$X\longrightarrow\prod_{1\leq i\leq k}\partial_{\leq 2}\mathcal{C}(Q_i)~.$$
    Then $\mathcal{H}$ is amenable.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- cr-2: body_md -->
\label{if IA or T everywhere, then amen. If nonamen then IN somewhere}
    If $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ stabilises a multicurve $\sigma$ and is trivial or irreducible amenable on every connected component of $\Sigma\setminus\sigma$, then $\mathcal{H}$ is amenable.
<!-- end cr-2 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
\label{amen is red or IA}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$. Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface and let $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ be a cocycle with trivial kernel. Let $\mathcal{A}$ be an infinite-type amenable subgroupoid of $\mathcal{G}$. Assume that the canonical reduction set of $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ exists. 
    
    Then, $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ is reducible or irreducible amenable on any subsurface that it stabilises. In other words, $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ is never irreducible nonamenable.
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
When $Q$ is stabilised by $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$, the cocycle $\rho$ sends $\mathcal{A}$ to $\mathrm{Stab}(\partial Q)$. This subgroup acts on the compact metrisable space $\mathcal{PMF}$ of projective measured foliations on the surface $Q$, as explained in \cite[Section~5.2]{kida_introduction_2009}. By \cite[Proposition~4.14]{kida_introduction_2009}, there exists a measurable $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$-equivariant map $$\varphi : X\longrightarrow M(\mathcal{PMF})$$ where $M(\mathcal{PMF})$ are the probability measures on $\mathcal{PMF}$. Then, by \cite[Theorem~5.3]{kida_introduction_2009}, there exists a partition $X= X_1\sqcup X_2$ of $X$ such that $\varphi|_{X_1}$ takes values in $M(\mathcal{MIN})$, where $\mathcal{MIN}$ are minimal measured foliations, and $\varphi|_{X_2}$ takes values in $M(\mathcal{PMF}\setminus \mathcal{MIN})$.

    On $X_1$, Kida uses the map $\mathcal{MIN}\longrightarrow\partial \mathcal{C}(Q)$ constructed by Klarreich in \cite{klarreich_boundary_2022} to get an equivariant map towards $M(\partial \mathcal{C}(Q))$. Then, \cite[Proposition~4.29]{kida_introduction_2009} states that this map has support of cardinality at most $2$, which makes $(\mathcal{A}|_{X_1},\rho)$ reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$. On $X_2$, Kida uses the fact that any foliation in $\mathcal{PMF}\setminus \mathcal{MIN}$ contains a canonical multicurve, which is then stabilised by $(\mathcal{A}|_{X_2},\rho)$, so the latter is reducible on $Q$. But the canonical reduction set of $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ exists. Thus, if $X_2$ has positive measure, then the whole $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ is reducible on $Q$. If $X_2$ has null measure, then $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ is reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{if H normalises A then its reducible or IA}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a measured groupoid over a standard probability space $X$. Let ${\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)}$ be a cocycle with trivial kernel. Let $\mathcal{A}$ and $\mathcal{H}$ be infinite-type subgroupoids of $\mathcal{G}$ such that $\mathcal{H}$ normalises $\mathcal{A}$. Assume that $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ and $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ both stabilise an open subsurface $Q$ and $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ has a canonical multicurve.
    
    Assume that $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ is nontrivial and reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$. Then, $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$.

    In particular, if $\mathcal{A}$ is amenable and nontrivial on $Q$, then $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$.
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
The subsurface $Q$ has positive complexity because $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q$.

If $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ is irreducible amenable on $Q$, then there exists a measurable and $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$-equivariant map ${X\longrightarrow \partial_{\leq 2}\mathcal{C}(Q)}$ that is maximal for inclusion, as shown in \cite[Lemma~5.8]{kida_introduction_2009}. This maximality is enough for it to also be equivariant by a subgroupoid that normalises $\mathcal{A}$, namely $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$, as shown in \cite[Lemma~6.7]{kida_introduction_2009}. This means that $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ is either reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$.

If $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ is reducible, let $\sigma$ be the canonical multicurve of $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$. By Proposition~\ref{reducible nontrivial implies existence of canonical curve}, $\sigma$ contains a curve of $\mathcal{C}(Q)$, which is stabilised by $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma multicurve is canonical}. This makes $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$ reducible.

In particular, if $\mathcal{A}$ is amenable then $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ is reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$ by Proposition~\ref{amen is red or IA}, so the latter holds.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- setting-1: body_md -->
\label{setting for thm}
We fix two connected orientable finite-type surfaces $\Sigma$ and $\Sigma'$ without boundary and of topological complexity at least $1$. Fix $H=\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ and $G=\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma')$ the finite-index subgroups of $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ and $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma')$ respectively as in Definition~\ref{def mapping class group, subgroups of subsurfaces, modz def}. Fix a measurable embedding coupling $\Omega$ of $H$ into $G$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma measurable embedding provides groupoid and cocycles} and rescale the measure on $\Omega$ to get a Borel subset $X$ of $\Omega$ of measure $1$ such that $\left(G\times H\right) X$ is conull in $\Omega$ and there is a measured groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ over the standard probability space $X$ with 
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep, nolistsep, label=-]
    \item an action-type cocycle $\omega :\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow H$, 
    \item a trivial-kernel cocycle $\rho : \mathcal{G}\longrightarrow G$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end setting-1 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{adjacency curves in groupoidal terms}
Under Setting~\ref{setting for thm}, let $\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}$ be infinite-type subgroupoids of $\mathcal{G}$ and $\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma')$. Assume that $\rho(\mathcal{A})\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha}} \rangle$ and $\rho(\mathcal{B})\subseteq \langle {T_{\beta}} \rangle$.
\begin{itemize}
    \item If $\alpha,\beta$ are disjoint, then $\langle {\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}} \rangle$ is amenable.
    \item If $\alpha,\beta$ are not disjoint, then $\langle {\mathcal{A}|_U,\mathcal{B}|_U} \rangle$ is everywhere nonamenable, for every Borel subset $U\subseteq X$ of positive measure.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
If $\alpha,\beta$ are disjoint curves, then $\langle {\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}} \rangle$ is sent by $\rho$ into $\langle {T_{\alpha},T_{\beta}} \rangle$ which is amenable. The cocycle having trivial kernel, this forces $\langle {\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}} \rangle$ to be amenable by Lemma~\ref{lemma triv kernel cocycle amenable group amenable groupoid}.

    If $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are not disjoint, then $\langle {T_{\alpha}} \rangle\cap \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma')$ and $\langle {T_{\beta}} \rangle\cap \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma')$ generate a nonabelian free group by Corollary~\ref{prop in modz all twists are free groups}. Then, apply Proposition~\ref{lemma A,B in F2 nonamenable} to 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{A})\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha}} \rangle\cap \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma')$$
    and 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{B})\subseteq \langle {T_{\beta}} \rangle\cap \mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma')$$
    to obtain that for every positive measure subset $U$ of $X$, $\langle {\mathcal{A}|_U,\mathcal{B}|_U} \rangle$ is everywhere nonamenable.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lemma b intersects S}
Under Setting~\ref{setting for thm}, let $S$ be a connected open subsurface of $\Sigma$ with $\kappa(S)\geq 1$ and let $b$ be a curve of $\Sigma$ that intersects $S$ nontrivially. Let $U$ be a positive measure Borel subset of $X$. Assume that there exists an open subsurface $Q$ of $\Sigma'$ and a multicurve $\sigma$ of $\Sigma'\setminus Q$ such that 
$$\rho(\omega^{-1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{ S }(\Sigma))|_U)\subseteq\mathrm{Mod}_{ Q }(\Sigma)\times \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma\setminus \partial Q}~.$$
Assume also that there exists a multicurve $\beta$ such that
$$\rho(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_b} \rangle)|_U)\subseteq  \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\beta}~.$$


Then, $\beta$ intersects $Q$ nontrivially. In particular, if the canonical multicurve of $(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_b} \rangle)|_U,\rho)$ is $\beta$, then $(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_b} \rangle)|_U,\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
By Koberda's \cite[Theorem~1.1]{koberda_right-angled_2012}, if three curves pairwise intersect each other, then a big enough power of the associated Dehn twists generate a free group. Thus, using that $\kappa(S)\geq 1$, take $a$ and $a'$ two curves of $\mathcal{C}(S)$ such that there exists an integer $k$ such that $T_a^k,T_{a'}^k$ and $T_b^k$ generate a free group $F_3$. For instance, $a$ and $a'$ can be obtained by taking two intersecting curves on $S$ and applying a big enough power of a pseudo-Anosov of $S$ to these curves.

    Now, denote by 
    \begin{align*}
        \mathcal{B}^0=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_b^k} \rangle)|_U\, ,\\
        \mathcal{A}^0=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_a^k} \rangle)|_U\, ,\\
        \mathcal{A}'^0=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{a'}^k} \rangle)|_U
    \end{align*}

    and note that $\langle {\mathcal{A}^0,\mathcal{A}'^0} \rangle$ is everywhere nonamenable by Lemma~\ref{lemme action-type groupe non moyennable donne groupoide non moyennable}. Moreover, note that $\langle {\mathcal{A}^0,\mathcal{A}'^0} \rangle\subseteq \omega^{-1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{ S }(\Sigma))|_U$ which in particular means that 
        $$\rho \left(\langle {\mathcal{A}^0,\mathcal{A}'^0} \rangle\right) \subseteq \mathrm{Mod}_{ Q }(\Sigma')\times \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma\setminus \partial Q}~.$$
    Define the projection $$\bar\rho :\langle {\mathcal{A}^0,\mathcal{A}'^0} \rangle\longrightarrow \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma\setminus \partial Q}$$ and define the subgroupoid $\mathcal{K}$ as the kernel of $\bar\rho$, i.e.\
    $$ \mathcal{K}=\{\mathfrak g\in \langle {\mathcal{A}^0,\mathcal{A}'^0} \rangle~|~ \rho(\mathfrak g)\in\mathrm{Mod}_{ Q }(\Sigma')\}~.$$

    Let us show that $\mathcal{K}$ is of infinite type. For $n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}$, the Borel subsets
    $$U_n=\{x\in U~|~\#\mathcal{K}_{x}=n\}$$
    give the partition
    $$U=\bigsqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}U_n~.$$
    Each $U_n$ for $n\in\mathbb{N}$ can be cut up into $n$ Borel pieces such that $\mathcal{K}$ is trivial when restricted to each piece. Therefore, 
    $$U=U_{\infty}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}} V_m$$
    such that $\mathcal{K}|_{V_m}$ is trivial for all $m$. But if $V_m$ has positive measure for some $m$, then $$\bar\rho : \langle {\mathcal{A}^0,\mathcal{A}'^0} \rangle|_{V_m}\longrightarrow\langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma\setminus \partial Q}$$ is a cocycle with trivial kernel that sends a nonamenable subgroupoid to an amenable group. Therefore, by Lemma~\ref{lemma triv kernel cocycle amenable group amenable groupoid}, $V_m$ has measure zero for all $m$. Thus $U_{\infty}$ is conull in $U$ which exactly means that $\mathcal{K}$ is of infinite type.

    Assume that $\beta$ intersects $Q$ trivially. Then, every curve of $\beta$ is disjoint from $Q$. This means that the multitwist $\langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\beta}$ is centralised by $\mathrm{Mod}_{ Q }(\Sigma')$. In other words, if we denote $$G=\mathrm{Mod}_{ Q }(\Sigma')\times \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\beta\setminus \partial Q}~,$$then $$\langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\beta}\subseteq Z(G)~.$$
    Thus, 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{K})\subseteq G\text{ and }\rho(\mathcal{B}^0)\subseteq Z(G)$$
    but
    $$\omega(\mathcal{K})\subseteq \langle {T_{a}^k,T_{a'}^k} \rangle\text{ and }\omega(\mathcal{B}^0)\subseteq \langle {T_{b}^k} \rangle$$
    and we chose the curves $a,a'$ and $b$ to verify $$\langle {T_{a}^k,T_{a'}^k,T_{b}^k} \rangle=\langle {T_{a}^k,T_{a'}^k} \rangle\ast\langle {T_{b}^k} \rangle$$
    This contradicts Lemma~\ref{lemma free product into direct product}, hence $\beta$ intersects $Q$ nontrivially.
    
    In particular, if $\beta$ is the canonical reduction multicurve of $(\mathcal{B},\rho)$ and if $(\mathcal{B},\rho)$ were trivial on $Q$, then $Q$ would be contained in the subsurface filled by the canonical reduction set of $(\mathcal{B},\rho)$. This would force $\beta$ to be disjoint from the open subsurface $Q$, which is not the case here.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}

Under Setting~\ref{setting for thm}, take a $k$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$ and denote by $S_1,\dots,S_n$ the corresponding connected components and $\bar b =(b_1,\dots,b_{n-1})$ the corresponding multicurve, with $n={n_{\Sigma}}-k$.
For $1\leq i\leq n$ and $1\leq j< n$, let
\begin{align*}
\mathcal{S}_i = \omega^{-1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{ S_i }(\Sigma))\\
\mathcal{B}_j = \omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{b_j}} \rangle)~.
\end{align*}

Then, it holds that $\kappa(\Sigma)\leq \kappa(\Sigma')$. 

Assume additionally that $\Sigma=\Sigma'$. Then, there exists a countable conull partition of $X$ into Borel subsets such that for every piece $U$ of positive measure, there exists a $k$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$ into connected components $Q_1,\dots,Q_n$ by a multicurve $\sigma$ such that the following hold.

\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
    \item \label{i max decompos}For all $i\le n$, $\kappa(S_i)=\kappa(Q_i)$ and $({\mathcal{S}_i}|_U,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on $Q_i$ and trivial on $Q_j$ for every $j\neq i$. In particular, 
    $$\rho({\mathcal{S}_i}|_U)\subseteq\mathrm{Mod}_{ Q_i }(\Sigma)\times \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma\setminus \partial Q_i}~.$$

    \item \label{ii max decompos}For all $j<n$, $$\rho({\mathcal{B}_j}|_U)\subseteq\langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma}~.$$
    \item \label{iii max decompos}The map
    $$f_{\bar b,\sigma} : \begin{array}{ccl}
     V(\Gamma_{\bar b})&\longrightarrow &V(\Gamma_{\sigma}) \\
     S_i&\longmapsto & Q_i
\end{array}$$
    is a graph isomorphism. In other words, for any $i,j\le n$, 
    $S_i$ and $S_j$ share a boundary curve if and only if $Q_i$ and $Q_j$ do.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- rem-11: body_md -->
\label{rem on equality or not of S and S'}
