    The first half of the proof of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} (Points~\ref{1 proof} through \ref{5 proof}) holds when $\Sigma$ and $\Sigma'$ are not necessarily equal and implies monotonicity of topological complexity, used to prove Corollary~\ref{cr monotonicity of complexity} in Section~\ref{conclusion subsection}. It is the only result we show in the full generality of Setting~\ref{setting for thm}. After proving $\kappa(\Sigma)\leq \kappa(\Sigma')$, we will always assume that $\Sigma=\Sigma'$.
<!-- end rem-11 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
By definition of $k$-maximal decompositions (see Definition~\ref{def kmax decompo}), choose the numbering of the $(S_i)_i$ such that the surfaces $S_1,\dots,S_{2k+\delta_{\Sigma}}$ have topological complexity $2$ and the surfaces $S_{2k+\delta_{\Sigma}+1},\dots,S_n$ have topological complexity $1$. 
    
    On $S_i$ for $i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$, let us choose five curves $a_i^{(1)}, \dots,a_i^{(5)}$ that have the following property: each of these five curves is disjoint from two curves of these five that intersect each other. Figure~\ref{fig five curves} shows such a choice of curves on a twice-punctured torus and a $5$-punctured sphere. On $S_i$ for $i>2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$, simply take curves $a_i^{(1)}$ and $a_i^{(2)}$ that intersect. Denote $$\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)}=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{a_i^{(t)}}} \rangle)$$ for all these curves.
    \begin{figure}[ht]
	\centering
	\includesvg[width=0.9\textwidth]{fivecurves}
    \caption{Curves $a_i^{(1)}, \dots,a_i^{(5)}$ on some component $S_i$ of complexity $2$.}
	\label{fig five curves}
\end{figure}

    Fix a conull partition of $X$ adapted to the canonical multicurves of $\mathcal{S}_i$, $\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)}$, and $\mathcal{B}_j$ for all $i,j$ with respect to the cocycle $\rho$. It is always possible by Lemma~\ref{canonical reduction set exists} and Remark~\ref{rem we can always choose U for the canonical multicurve to exist}. For a piece $Y$ of that partition of positive measure, refine it into a new partition adapted to $$\langle {\mathcal{A}_i^{(1)}|_Y,\dots,\mathcal{A}_i^{(5)}|_Y} \rangle\text{ for }i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$$ and $$\langle {\mathcal{A}_i^{(1)}|_Y,\mathcal{A}_i^{(2)}|_Y} \rangle\text{ for }i>2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$$ with respect to $\rho$.
    Take a piece $U$ of that refined partition and define $$\mathcal{R}_i\mathrel{\mathop:}=\langle {\mathcal{A}_i^{(1)}|_U,\dots,\mathcal{A}_i^{(5)}|_U} \rangle\text{ for }i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$$ and $$\mathcal{R}_i\mathrel{\mathop:}=\langle {\mathcal{A}_i^{(1)}|_U,\mathcal{A}_i^{(2)}|_U} \rangle\text{ for }i>2k+\delta_{\Sigma}~.$$ From now on and until the end of the proof of this lemma, we can assume that $X=U$ and thus that all the canonical multicurves of all the subgroupoids $\mathcal{S}_i$, $\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)}$, $\mathcal{B}_j$, $$\mathcal{R}_i=\langle {\mathcal{A}_i^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{A}_i^{(5)}} \rangle\text{ for }i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$$ and $$\mathcal{R}_i=\langle {\mathcal{A}_i^{(1)},\mathcal{A}_i^{(2)}} \rangle\text{ for }i>2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$$
    with respect to $\rho$ exist.
    
\medbreak
    Let us now list all the information we get on our subgroupoids from their definition via the action-type cocycle $\omega$. Outside of this list, we will not use the cocycle $\omega$ in the proof of this lemma.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item For all $i\leq n$, $\mathcal{R}_i$ and $\mathcal{S}_i$ are everywhere nonamenable by Lemma~\ref{lemme action-type groupe non moyennable donne groupoide non moyennable}.
        \item For all $j<n$, $\mathcal{B}_j$ is amenable by Lemma~\ref{lemma triv kernel cocycle amenable group amenable groupoid}.
        \item For all $i\neq j\leq n$, $\mathcal{S}_i$ and $\mathcal{R}_i$ normalise $\mathcal{S}_j,\mathcal{R}_j$ and $\mathcal{A}_j^{(t)}$ for any well-defined $t$ (i.e.\ any $t\leq 5$ if $i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$ and any $t\leq 2$ if $i>2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$), by Lemma~\ref{lemma if groups normalise then groupoids normalise}.
        \item For all $i\leq n$ and $j<n$, $\mathcal{B}_j$ and $\mathcal{R}_i$ normalise each other, and $\mathcal{B}_j$ and $\mathcal{S}_i$ normalise each other, again by Lemma~\ref{lemma if groups normalise then groupoids normalise}.
        \item For every $i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$ and every $t\leq 5$, there exist $s,s'\leq 5$ such that $i(a_i^{(s)},a_i^{(s')})\geq 1$ and $a_i^{(t)}$ is distinct and disjoint from both $a_i^{(s)}$ and $a_i^{(s')}$. Then, it holds that
        \begin{itemize}[noitemsep, nolistsep, label=-]
            \item $\langle {\mathcal{A}_i^{(s)},\mathcal{A}_i^{(s')}} \rangle$ is everywhere nonamenable by Lemma~\ref{adjacency curves in groupoidal terms};
            \item $\langle {\mathcal{A}_i^{(s)},\mathcal{A}_i^{(s')}} \rangle$ and $\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)}$ normalise each other by Lemma~\ref{lemma if groups normalise then groupoids normalise};
            \item $\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)}$ is amenable by Lemma~\ref{lemma triv kernel cocycle amenable group amenable groupoid}.
        \end{itemize}
    \end{itemize}

    We first study the subgroupoids $\mathcal{R}_i$ and obtain monotonicity of $\kappa$.
    Let $\sigma_i$ be the canonical multicurve of $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ for every $i$. For every $j\neq i$, $\mathcal{R}_i$ normalises $\mathcal{R}_j$ so by Lemma~\ref{lemma multicurve is canonical}, $\sigma_i$ and $\sigma_j$ are disjoint and $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ stabilises $\bigcup_{j\neq i}\sigma_j$. Therefore, every $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ stabilises the whole multicurve
    $$\sigma=\bigcup_i\sigma_i~.$$

     \begin{enumerate}[(1)]
        \item\label{1 proof}  Let $Q$ be a connected component of $\Sigma'\setminus \sigma$. If $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is nontrivial and reducible on $Q$, then there would be a curve of $\sigma_i$ on $Q$ by Proposition~\ref{reducible nontrivial implies existence of canonical curve}, which is not the case. So $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is either trivial or irreducible on $Q$.
        \item \label{2 proof}For any $i\leq n$, since $\mathcal{R}_i$ is everywhere nonamenable, Corollary~\ref{if IA or T everywhere, then amen. If nonamen then IN somewhere} ensures that $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on at least one connected component $Q$ of $\Sigma'\setminus \sigma$. In particular, there necessarily exists a $t$ such that $(\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)},\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q$, otherwise $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ would be trivial on $Q$. Note that the latter statement is the reason why we first study $\mathcal{R}_i$ before we can study $\mathcal{S}_i$.
        \item\label{3 proof} Fix $i\neq j$ and take $Q$ a connected component of $\Sigma'\setminus\sigma$ on which $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable. Let us show that $(\mathcal{R}_j,\rho)$ is forced to be trivial on $Q$. Fix $t$ such that $(\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)},\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q$ by Point~\ref{2 proof}. The subgroupoid $\mathcal{R}_j$ normalises the amenable subgroupoid $\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)}$ so by Proposition~\ref{if H normalises A then its reducible or IA}, $(\mathcal{R}_j,\rho)$ is either reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$. If $(\mathcal{R}_j,\rho)$ were nontrivial on $Q$, apply Proposition~\ref{if H normalises A then its reducible or IA} to $\mathcal{R}_i$ normalising $\mathcal{R}_j$. This would make $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$, which is a contradiction. So $(\mathcal{R}_j,\rho)$ is trivial on $Q$.
    
        \item \label{4 proof}For $i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$, let us show that $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is either irreducible nonamenable on some connected component $Q$ of $\Sigma'\setminus\sigma$ of topological complexity $\kappa(Q)\geq 2$ or it is irreducible nonamenable on at least two connected components of complexity at least $1$. The case to eliminate here is that $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on exactly one connected component $Q$ of complexity $1$ and irreducible amenable on some other connected components $Q_1,\dots ,Q_m$, where $m$ can be zero. So let us assume this situation and arrive to a contradiction. 

        Up to renumbering, $(\mathcal{A}_i^{(1)},\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q$ by Point~\ref{2 proof} and is normalised by the everywhere nonamenable groupoid $$\mathcal{H}_i\mathrel{\mathop:}=\langle {\mathcal{A}_i^{(2)},\mathcal{A}_i^{(3)}} \rangle~.$$

        Note that by Point~\ref{1 proof}, $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is trivial on all the components that are not $Q_1,\dots,Q_m$, $Q$. Then, $(\mathcal{H}_i,\rho)$ is also trivial on these components because $\mathcal{H}_i$ is a subgroupoid of $\mathcal{R}_i$. 
        
        For the components $Q_1,\dots,Q_m$, there exists a measurable and $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$-equivariant map 
        $$\varphi : X\longrightarrow \partial_{\leq2}\mathcal{C}(Q_1)\times \cdots\times \partial_{\leq 2}\mathcal{C}(Q_m)~.$$
        by definition of irreducible amenability. This map is in particular $(\mathcal{H}_i,\rho)$-equivariant.
        
        It is left to study $(\mathcal{H}_i,\rho)$ on $Q$. The subgroupoid $\mathcal{H}_i$ normalises the amenable subgroupoid $\mathcal{A}_i^{(1)}$ so Proposition~\ref{if H normalises A then its reducible or IA} gives that $(\mathcal{H}_i,\rho)$ is either reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q$.
        \begin{itemize}
            \item If $(\mathcal{H}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible amenable on $Q$, then there exists an $(\mathcal{H}_i,\rho)$-equivariant map $$\psi: X \longrightarrow\partial_{\leq2}\mathcal{C}(Q)~.$$
            Then, Lemma~\ref{map to product of boundaries of curve graphs of components} applies to the multicurve $\sigma$ and the map $\varphi\times\psi$ and makes $\mathcal{H}_i$ amenable.
            
            \item If $(\mathcal{H}_i,\rho)$ fixes a curve $c$ on $Q$, then 
            $$\rho(\mathcal{H}_i)\subseteq \mathrm{Stab}(\sigma\sqcup\{c\})$$
            but $Q$ having complexity $1$ means that $Q\setminus c$ is one or two subsurfaces of complexity $0$ so $(\mathcal{H}_i,\rho)$ is trivial on $Q\setminus \{c\}$. Hence Lemma~\ref{map to product of boundaries of curve graphs of components} applies to the multicurve $\sigma\sqcup \{c\}$ and the map $\varphi$ and makes $\mathcal{H}_i$ amenable.
        \end{itemize}
        In either case, we have shown that $\mathcal{H}_i$ is amenable by applying Kida's Lemma~\ref{map to product of boundaries of curve graphs of components}. But $\mathcal{H}_i$ is an everywhere nonamenable subgroupoid, hence the contradiction.

        \item\label{5 proof} Let us consider the dual graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}=(V,E)$ associated to the multicurve $\sigma$ of $\Sigma'$. Note that we have not yet eliminated the case where a curve of $\sigma$ is the boundary of only one component, which is represented by a loop in the graph. Recall (\ref{equation star}) from Remark~\ref{rem equation star}
        $$\kappa(\Sigma')+1=2\#V+\sum_{v\in V}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right) + b_1~.$$
        Denote by $I$ the following set of indices
        $$I\mathrel{\mathop:}= \{i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}~|~ \exists v,v'\in V,~ v\neq v',~(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho) \text{ is irreducible nonamenable on }v\text{ and }v'\}~.$$
        For $i\neq j\leq n$, if $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on a component $v$ and $(\mathcal{R}_j,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on a component $v'$, then $v\neq v'$ by Point~\ref{3 proof}. Thus every $i\leq n$ contributes at least $1$ to $\#V$ and every $i\in I$ contributes at least $2$, which gives
        \begin{equation}\label{inequality on card V}
            \#V\geq {n_{\Sigma}} -k+\# I
        \end{equation}
        because $n={n_{\Sigma}}-k$.

        Point~\ref{4 proof} tells us that for $i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$, if $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on only one component $v$, then necessarily $\kappa(v)\geq 2$. Therefore, if $i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$ but $i\notin I$, then it contributes at least $1$ to the sum $\sum_{v\in V}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)$, which gives
        \begin{equation}\label{inequality on sum complexities}
            \sum_{v\in V}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right)\ge 2k+\delta_{\Sigma} -\# I~.
        \end{equation}
        Summing twice the inequality (\ref{inequality on card V}) with the inequality (\ref{inequality on sum complexities}) and using $\# I\geq 0$ and $b_1\geq 0$, we get
        \begin{align}\label{ineq kappa}
           \nonumber \kappa(\Sigma')+1&=2\#V+\sum_{v\in V}\left(\kappa(v)-1\right) + b_1\\ \nonumber
                        &\geq 2{n_{\Sigma}}-2k+2\#I+2k+\delta_{\Sigma} -\#I+b_1\\\nonumber
                        &= 2{n_{\Sigma}}+\delta_{\Sigma}  +\#I+b_1\\
                        &\geq 2{n_{\Sigma}}+\delta_{\Sigma} = \kappa(\Sigma)+1~.
        \end{align}
        We have shown that $\kappa(\Sigma)\leq \kappa(\Sigma')$.
        \end{enumerate}

        \textbf{From now on, assume that $\Sigma=\Sigma'$.} We first show Point~\ref{i max decompos} of the lemma for the subgroupoids $\mathcal{R}_i$ and then we will deduce it for the $\mathcal{S}_i$. 
        
        Now that $\Sigma=\Sigma'$, the inequalities of (\ref{ineq kappa}) are all equalities. This forces the inequalities (\ref{inequality on card V}), (\ref{inequality on sum complexities}) as well as $\# I\geq 0$ and $b_1\geq 0$ to all be equalities.
        \begin{itemize}[-]
            \item The fact that $b_1=0$ means that the graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ is a tree.
            \item The fact that $\# I=0$ means that for every $i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$, $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on exactly one component that we denote $Q_i$.
            \item The fact that (\ref{inequality on card V}) is an equality means that for every $j> 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$, $(\mathcal{R}_j,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on exactly one component that we denote $Q_j$.
            \item The fact that (\ref{inequality on sum complexities}) is an equality means that $\kappa(Q_i)=2$ for every $i\leq 2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$ and $\kappa(Q_j)=1$ for every $j>2k+\delta_{\Sigma}$.
        \end{itemize}
        To sum up, we have shown that $\sigma$ is a $k$-maximal decomposition of $\Sigma$ with connected components $Q_1,\dots,Q_{2k+\delta_{\Sigma}}$ of complexity $2$ and $Q_{2k+\delta_{\Sigma}+1},\dots,Q_n$ of complexity $1$. Moreover, for every $i\leq n$, $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on $Q_i$ and trivial on $Q_j$ for every $j\neq i$. In other words, we have shown Point~\ref{i max decompos} for the subgroupoids $\mathcal{R}_i$.
     

     Now, let us show Point~\ref{i max decompos} for the subgroupoids $\mathcal{S}_i$. Fix $1\le i\neq j \le n$.
     \begin{itemize}[nolistsep, noitemsep]
        \item The subgroupoid $(\mathcal{S}_i,\rho)$ cannot be trivial on $Q_i$ because it contains $\mathcal{R}_i$.
        \item If $(\mathcal{S}_i,\rho)$ were reducible on $Q_i$, then there would exist a curve $c$ on $Q_i$ such that 
        $$\rho(\mathcal{S}_i)\subseteq \mathrm{Stab}(c)$$
        but $\mathcal{R}_i\subseteq\mathcal{S}_i$, so
        $$\rho(\mathcal{R}_i)\subseteq \mathrm{Stab}(c)$$
        which contradicts the irreducibility of $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ on $Q_i$. Thus $(\mathcal{S}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible on $Q_i$.
        \item If $(\mathcal{S}_i,\rho)$ were irreducible amenable on $Q_i$, then the associated $(\mathcal{S}_i,\rho)$-equivariant map would also be $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$-equivariant because $\mathcal{R}_i\subseteq \mathcal{S}_i$. This would contradict the fact that $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable. Thus $(\mathcal{S}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on $Q_i$.
        \item Fix $t$ such that $(\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)},\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q_i$, which exists by Point~\ref{2 proof}. The subgroupoid $\mathcal{S}_j$ normalises the amenable subgroupoid $\mathcal{A}_i^{(t)}$ so by Proposition~\ref{if H normalises A then its reducible or IA}, $(\mathcal{S}_j,\rho)$ is either reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q_i$. If $(\mathcal{S}_j,\rho)$ were nontrivial on $Q_i$, apply Proposition~\ref{if H normalises A then its reducible or IA} to $\mathcal{S}_i$ normalising $\mathcal{S}_j$. This gives a contradiction with the fact that $(\mathcal{S}_i,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on $Q_i$. So $(\mathcal{S}_j,\rho)$ is trivial on $Q_i$.
     \end{itemize}
     This concludes the proof of Point~\ref{i max decompos}.

\medbreak
     Now, let us prove Point~\ref{ii max decompos}. Fix $j<n$. Note that $\mathcal{B}_j$ normalises $\mathcal{R}_i$ for all $i\le n$, so $(\mathcal{B}_j,\rho)$ stabilises $\sigma$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma multicurve is canonical}. By contradiction, assume that $(\mathcal{B}_j,\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q_i$ for some $i\le n$. It is amenable and normalised by $\mathcal{R}_i$. By Proposition~\ref{if H normalises A then its reducible or IA}, this makes $(\mathcal{R}_i,\rho)$ reducible or irreducible amenable on $Q_i$ which contradicts the definition of $Q_i$. Therefore, $(\mathcal{B}_j,\rho)$ is trivial on every component of $\Sigma\setminus\sigma$ so $\mathcal{B}_j$ is sent by $\rho$ into the multitwist generated by the curves of $\sigma$. This concludes the proof of Point~\ref{ii max decompos}.
\medbreak
     Finally, we show Point~\ref{iii max decompos}. 
    Take $\Gamma_{\bar b}$ the graph dual to the decomposition $\bar b$, which has vertices $(S_i)_{i\leq n}$. Similarly, $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ the graph dual to $\sigma$ has vertices $(Q_i)_{i\leq n}$. The map $f_{\bar b,\sigma}$
    that sends $S_i$ to $Q_i$ is injective by Point~\ref{i max decompos}. 

     Now, let us show that $f_{\bar b,\sigma}$ sends two adjacent vertices to two adjacent vertices. Up to renumbering, assume that $S_1$ and $S_2$ share a boundary curve $b_1$ of the decomposition $\bar b$. Choose a curve $b'$ on $S_1\cup\{b_1\}\cup S_2$ that intersects both $S_1$ and $S_2$ nontrivially and such that the two components of $$\left(S_1\cup\{b_1\}\cup S_2 \right)\setminus b'~,$$ denoted $S_1'$ and $S_2'$, verify $\kappa(S_1)=\kappa(S_1')$ and $\kappa(S_2)=\kappa(S_2')$. 
     Then, $\bar b'=(b',b_2,\dots,b_{n-1})$ is still a $k$-maximal decomposition. 
     Restrict to a smaller positive measure subset $V$ of $U$ to apply Points~\ref{i max decompos} and \ref{ii max decompos} to this new decomposition $\bar b'$. Name $\sigma'$ the image multicurve and $Q_1',\dots,Q_n'$ the components of the image decomposition. 
     
      For $i\leq n$, the subsurface $Q_i$ (resp. $Q_i'$) is the unique subsurface of $\Sigma$ on which $(\mathcal{S}_i|_V,\rho)$ (resp. $(\mathcal{S}_i'|_V,\rho)$) is irreducible nonamenable, by Point~\ref{i max decompos}. Thus, the definition of $Q_i$ only depends on the subsurface $S_i$. But recall that the two decompositions $(S_i)_i$ and $(S_i')_i$ at the source differ only by the first two subsurfaces $S_1$ and $S_2$. Thus, for $i\geq 3$, $$\mathcal{S}_i|_V=\mathcal{S}_i'|_V$$ so $(\mathcal{S}_i|_V,\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on both $Q_i$ and $Q_i'$. Therefore, $Q_i=Q_i'$ for all $i\geq 3$.

    Assume by contradiction that $Q_1$ and $Q_2$ do not share any boundary curve. This means that
    $$\sigma=\bigcup_{i\geq 3} \partial Q_i = \bigcup_{i\geq 3} \partial Q_i '= \sigma'~.$$
    Denote by $\mathcal{B}'=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{b'}} \rangle)$. By Point~\ref{ii max decompos} for the decomposition $\bar b'$,  
    $$\rho(\mathcal{B}'|_V)\subseteq\langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma'}~.$$
    But here we have $\sigma=\sigma'$, so 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{B}'|_V)\subseteq\langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma}~.$$
    The subset $V$ is chosen small enough for the canonical multicurve $\beta'$ of $(\mathcal{B}'|_V,\rho)$ to exist. By the latter, $\beta'\subseteq \sigma$. However, $b'$ intersects $S_1$ and $S_2$ nontrivially so by Lemma~\ref{lemma b intersects S}, $\beta'$ intersects both $Q_1$ and $Q_2$ nontrivially, which contradicts $\beta'\subseteq \sigma$.

    We have shown that $f_{\bar b,\sigma}$ sends two adjacent vertices of $\Gamma_{\bar b}$ to two adjacent vertices of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$. But these two graphs are finite trees with the same number $n$ of vertices. This means that the map $f_{\bar b,\sigma}$ is a graph isomorphism.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
\label{prop recap courbes dans courbes}
    Under Setting~\ref{setting for thm}, assume that $\Sigma=\Sigma'$ and $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 2$. Let $a\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ and let $\mathcal{A}=\omega^{-1}\left(\langle {T_a} \rangle\right)$. Then, there exists a countable conull Borel partition $\displaystyle \bigsqcup_m X_m$ of $X$ such that for each $m$, there exists $\alpha_m\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ such that 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{A}|_{X_m})\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha_m}} \rangle~.$$
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- prop-10: body_md -->
\label{prop leaf type curves}
    Under Setting~\ref{setting for thm}, assume that $\Sigma=\Sigma'$ and $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 3$. Let $b\in\mathcal{C}_{leaf}$ and let $\mathcal{B}=\omega^{-1}\left(\langle {T_b} \rangle\right)$. Then, there exists a countable conull Borel partition $\bigsqcup_m X_m$ of $X$ such that for each $m$, there exists $\beta_m\in\mathcal{C}_{leaf}$ such that 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{B}|_{X_m})\subseteq \langle {T_{\beta_m}} \rangle~.$$
<!-- end prop-10 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
When $\kappa(\Sigma)\in\{3,4\}$, ${n_{\Sigma}}=2$ so any single leaf curve is a $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve of $\Sigma$. Thus, apply Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} directly to the $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve $\{b\}$, fix a piece $Y\subseteq X$ of the partition given by Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} and get a $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve $\sigma=\{\beta\}$ composed of one unique leaf curve $\beta$ and verifying $\rho(\mathcal{B}|_{Y})\subseteq \langle {T_{\beta}} \rangle$. This concludes the proof in the case $\kappa(\Sigma)\leq 4$. 

    From now on, assume that $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 5$.
    \begin{Claim}\label{claim at most one leaf curve}
   Assume that $Y$ is a Borel subset of positive measure such that the canonical multicurve $\beta$ of $(\mathcal{B}|_Y,\rho)$ exists. Then, $\beta$ contains at most one leaf curve.
    \end{Claim}
    \begin{proof}[Proof of Claim~\ref{claim at most one leaf curve}]
    Any leaf curve can be completed into a $0$-maximal decomposition, see for instance the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop D les decompos particulieres avec g+p/3} for a concrete construction. Apply Point~\ref{ii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} to get that $\mathcal{B}|_{Z}$ is sent by $\rho$ into a multitwist for a small enough positive measure subset $Z\subseteq Y$. In other words, $(\mathcal{B}|_Z,\rho)$ is trivial on every connected component of $\Sigma\setminus \beta$, so $$\rho\left(\mathcal{B}|_Z\right)\subseteq \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\beta}~.$$
          
    First, let us build everything we need on the surface at the source of the measurable embedding. Complete $b$ into a $0$-maximal decomposition $(b_1,\dots,b_{n-1})$ where $b_1=b$ and denote $S_1,\dots,S_n$ the components of this decomposition, where $S_1$ is an open leaf subsurface separated from $\Sigma$ by $b$. Recall that this means that $n={n_{\Sigma}}$ and all the curves $b_j$ are separating. Now, complete the leaf curve $b$ into another $0$-maximal decomposition $(b_1',b_2',\dots,b_{n-1}')$ where $b_1'=b$ and such that for all $i,j\ge 2$, $b_j'$ intersects the open subsurface $S_i$ nontrivially. Denote $\mathcal{B}_j=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{b_j}} \rangle)$ and $\mathcal{B}_j'=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{b'_j}} \rangle)$.

    Now, let us give notations to what happens on the target copy of $\Sigma$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}, restrict to a small enough Borel subset $U$ of positive measure, and denote by $\sigma$ (resp. $\sigma'$) the $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve at the target that we get from the decomposition $(b_j)_j$ (resp. $(b_j')_j$). For every $i\leq n$, denote by $Q_i$ the component of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma$ on which $\omega^{-1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{ S_i }(\Sigma))|_U$ is irreducible nonamenable. Similarly, $Q_i'$ is the component of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma'$ on which $\left(\omega^{-1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{ S_i' }(\Sigma))|_U,\rho\right)$ is irreducible nonamenable.
    Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma b intersects S} to $b=b_j'$, $S=S_i$ and $Q=Q_i$ to obtain that $(\mathcal{B}_j'|_U,\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q_i$ for every $2\leq i\leq n$ and $2\leq j \leq n-1$.
    
    Assume by contradiction that $\beta$ contains two distinct leaf curves $a$ and $a'$. The multicurve $\beta$ is contained in both $\sigma$ and $\sigma'$, so $\{a,a'\}\subseteq \sigma\cap\sigma'$. This means that there are two leaf subsurfaces of the target $\Sigma$ that are a component in both decompositions. One of them is $Q_1=Q_1'$ coming from $S_1=S_1'$. But there is a second one, so there exist $i,i'\geq 2$ such that $\partial Q_i=\partial Q_{i'}'$ is a leaf curve of $\beta$, which forces the leaf subsurfaces $Q_i$ and $Q_{i'}'$ to be equal. For $j\geq 2$, by Point~\ref{ii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo},
    $$\rho\left(\mathcal{B}_j'|_U\right)\subseteq \langle {T_{c}} \rangle_{c\in\sigma'}\subseteq \mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q_{i'}' }^*}(\Sigma)$$
    and $Q_i=Q_{i'}'$ so $$\mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q_{i} }^*}(\Sigma)=\mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q_{i'}' }^*}(\Sigma)~.$$
    Therefore, 
    $$\rho\left(\mathcal{B}_j'|_U\right)\subseteq \mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q_{i} }^*}(\Sigma)$$
    which contradicts the fact that $(\mathcal{B}_j'|_U,\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q_i$.
    \end{proof}

    Let us now prove that preimages by $\omega$ of leaf curve twists cannot have any non-leaf curves in their canonical multicurve for $\rho$.
    For this, we take a step back and consider the collection $D$ of all the $0$-maximal decomposition multicurves $\sigma$ of $\Sigma$ that verify 
    $$\# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{leaf}\right) >\# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{nl}\right)~.$$
    To be clear, every element of $D$ is a multicurve of $n-1$ separating curves of $\Sigma$, where $n={n_{\Sigma}}$. By Proposition~\ref{prop D les decompos particulieres avec g+p/3}, any leaf curve of $\Sigma$ can be completed into a multicurve in $D$. 
    We will consider the preimages by $\omega$ of all the multicurves in $D$ and all their possible canonical multicurves for $\rho$.

    For every multicurve $\bar c\in D$, there exists a countable conull Borel partition $$X^*=\bigsqcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}} X_m^{\bar c}$$ such that the canonical multicurves of all the $(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{c}} \rangle)|_{X_m^{\bar c}},\rho)$, denoted $\gamma_m(c)$, exist for all $c\in\bar c$ and $m$, as explained in Remark~\ref{rem we can always choose U for the canonical multicurve to exist}. Given a multicurve $\bar c$ in $D$ and an integer $m$, define the following multicurve
    \begin{equation*}\label{def de sigma nl}
        \sigma_{nl}(\bar c ,X_m^{\bar c})=\mathcal{C}_{nl}\cap \bigcup_{c\in \bar c \,\cap\,\mathcal{C}_{leaf}}\gamma_m(c)~.\tag{Def$_{\sigma_{nl}}$}
    \end{equation*}
    It is the set of non-leaf curves that are in the image of some leaf curve of $\bar c$, on some positive measure subset of $X$.
\medbreak 
    The cardinality of a multicurve of $\Sigma$ is bounded, so we fix a $0$-maximal decomposition $\bar c$ and an $m\in\mathbb{N}$ such that $$\#\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})=\mathrel{\mathop:} N$$ is maximal amongst all possible values of $\sigma_{nl}$ on $D\times\mathbb{N}$. To prove the proposition, it suffices to show that $N=0$ because of Claim~\ref{claim at most one leaf curve}. So let us assume that $N\geq 1$ and aim towards a contradiction.
    
    To fix more notations, $\bar c = (c_1,\dots,c_{n-1})$ are separating curves that decompose the source $\Sigma$ into components $S_1,\dots,S_n$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} to the latter, restrict to a positive measure Borel subset $U$ of $X_m^{\bar c}$ and get the corresponding $0$-maximal decomposition $Q_1,\dots,Q_n$ by a multicurve $\sigma$ on the target surface $\Sigma$. Point~\ref{ii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} ensures that for every $c\in\bar c$, 
    $$\rho(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{c}} \rangle)|_{U})\subseteq \langle {T_a} \rangle_{a\in\sigma}$$
    so in particular it holds that $$\gamma_m(c)\subseteq \sigma~.$$Hence
    $$\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})\subseteq \sigma~.$$
    
    For simplicity, denote $\gamma_m(c_i)$ by $\gamma_i$ and remember that $\gamma_i\subseteq \sigma$.
    Renumber $\bar c$ so that $c_1,\dots,c_N$ are leaf curves that verify
    $$\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})=\mathcal{C}_{nl}\cap\bigcup_{i=1}^N \gamma_i~.$$
    In other words, we choose exactly $N$ leaf curves from $\bar c$ that give the whole $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$. Renumber the components $(S_i)_{i\leq n}$ of the decomposition $\bar c$ so that for $i\leq N$, $S_i$ is the component that $c_i$ separates from the rest. Also, note that $N<n$ because $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 5$ so there exist non-leaf components in the decomposition $(S_i)_{i\leq n}$.

    Now, define a new $0$-maximal decomposition $\bar c '$ by keeping the $c_1,\dots,c_N$ the same and choosing $c_{N+1}',\dots,c_{n-1}'$ that verify the following: for each $N<i\leq n$ and $N<j<n$, $c_j'$ intersects $S_i$ nontrivially. This is possible for instance by applying a pseudo-Anosov mapping class of the subsurface $\Sigma\setminus\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq N}S_i$, which has positive complexity because $N<n$ and is connected because all the components $S_i$ for $i\leq N$ correspond to leaves in the dual graph. Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} to this new decomposition $\bar c'$. Restrict  to a small enough Borel subset $V$ of $U$ of positive measure on which Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} holds, so that in particular the canonical multicurve $\gamma_j'$ of $(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{c_j'}} \rangle)|_V,\rho)$ exists. By Point~\ref{ii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}, $\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{c_j'}} \rangle)|_V$ is sent to a multitwist by $\rho$. Thus, by Lemma~\ref{lemma b intersects S} applied to $b=c_j'$, $S=S_i$ and $Q=Q_i$ for all $N<i\leq n$ and $N<j<n$, the multicurve $\gamma_j'$ intersects $Q_i$ nontrivially.

    Denote by $\sigma'$ the $0$-maximal decomposition we get at the target from $\bar c'$ on the Borel subset $V$, by Point~\ref{i max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}. The fact that $\bar c'$ contains $c_1,\dots,c_N$ guarantees that 
    $$\sigma'\supseteq \bigcup_{i=1}^N \gamma_i\supseteq \sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})~.$$
    But recall that for every $j<n$, $\gamma_j'\subseteq\sigma' $. In particular, this means that all the $\gamma_j'$ are disjoint from $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$. Thus, for every $j<n$, each curve of $\gamma_j'$ is either in $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$ or in $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma\setminus \sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c}))$. 

    \begin{Claim}\label{claim g_j' at least two curves}
        For $j>N$, $\gamma_j'$ contains at least two distinct curves in $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma\setminus \sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c}))$.
    \end{Claim}
    \begin{proof}[Proof of Claim~\ref{claim g_j' at least two curves}]
        
    Fix $j>N$. To prove this, we show that $\gamma_j'$ has curves on at least two distinct connected components of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$. Consider the dual tree $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ of the decomposition $\sigma$ as defined in Definition~\ref{def dual graph}. The edges of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ correspond to curves of $\sigma$. Let $E_{nl}$ be the edges of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ corresponding to $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$ and note that $\#E_{nl}=N$. Let $\Gamma_1,\dots,\Gamma_{N+1}$ be the connected components of the subforest of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ with vertices $V(\Gamma_{\sigma})$ and edges $E(\Gamma_{\sigma})\setminus E_{nl}$. Let us bound the size of these connected components. The tree $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ has at least two leaves, so up to renumbering, $\Gamma_1$ and $\Gamma_2$ contain a leaf. But $E_{nl}$ contains no edges that connect to a leaf, so $\#V(\Gamma_1),V(\Gamma_2)\geq 2$. The rest of the connected components verify $\#V(\Gamma_i)\geq 1$, which gives
    \begin{align*}
        \#V(\Gamma_1)&\leq \#V(\Gamma) - \#V(\Gamma_2)-\sum_{j=3}^{N+1} \#V(\Gamma_j)\\
                &\leq n -2 -\sum_{j=3}^{N+1} 1\\
                &\leq n-2-(N-1) = n-(N+1)
    \end{align*}
    and similarly $ \#V(\Gamma_2)\leq n-(N+1)$.
    For $i\leq 3$, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \#V(\Gamma_i)&\leq \#V(\Gamma) -\#V(\Gamma_1)- \#V(\Gamma_2)-\sum_{\substack{3\le j\le N+1\\ j\ne i}} \#V(\Gamma_j)\\
                &\leq n -2-2 -(N-2)\\
                &\leq n-(N+1)
    \end{align*}
    In conlusion, every connected component of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ with erased $E_{nl}$ edges has at most $n-(N+1)$ vertices.

    By Point~\ref{iii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}, the graph isomorphism $f_{\bar c,\sigma}$ sends the vertices $S_{N+1},\dots,S_n$ to vertices $Q_{N+1},\dots,Q_n$ inside of ${\Sigma \setminus\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})}$. In the point of view of dual graphs, this means exactly that $f_{\bar c,\sigma}$ is sending $n-(N+1)+1$ vertices of $\Gamma_{\bar c}$ into the subforest of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ with erased $E_{nl}$ edges. We just showed that each connected component of that subforest has at most $n-(N+1)$ vertices. Thus, there are two vertices sent by $f_{\bar c,\sigma}$ to two different connected components of the forest. This means that, up to renumbering, $Q_{N+1}$ and $Q_{N+2}$ are in different connected components of $\Sigma\setminus\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$. But $\gamma_j'$ is disjoint from $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$ and nontrivial on both $Q_{N+1}$ and $Q_{N+2}$. Therefore, $\gamma_j'$ contains at least two distinct curves that are in $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma\setminus \sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c}))$.
    \end{proof}

    Recall that the trees $\Gamma_{\bar c}$ and $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ are isomorphic by Point~\ref{iii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}. In particular, $\sigma$ and $\bar c$ have the same number of leaf and non-leaf curves, so 
    $$\#\left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{nl}\right)=\#\left(\bar c\cap \mathcal{C}_{nl}\right)~.$$
    Now, note that $$N=\#\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})\leq\# \left(\sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{nl}\right)$$
    because $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})\subseteq \sigma\cap \mathcal{C}_{nl}$. Moreover, $$\# \left(\bar c\cap \mathcal{C}_{nl}\right)<\# \left(\bar c\cap \mathcal{C}_{leaf}\right)$$ because $\bar c\in D$.
    Thus it holds that $$N<\# \left(\bar c\cap \mathcal{C}_{leaf}\right)~.$$
    This means that we can choose $j>N$ such that $c_j'$ is a leaf curve. By Claim~\ref{claim g_j' at least two curves}, $\gamma_j'$ contains at least two curves in $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma\setminus \sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c}))$. By definition, $\gamma_j'$ is the canonical multicurve of a leaf curve so by Claim~\ref{claim at most one leaf curve}, only one of these two can be a leaf curve. Therefore, the other one is a non-leaf curve that we denote $\alpha$. 
    Take the countable conull Borel partition $\sqcup_m X_m^{\bar c'}$ of $X$ used to define the map $\sigma_{nl}$ in (\ref{def de sigma nl}). Take $m'$ such that $V\cap X_{m'}^{\bar c'}$ has positive measure and note that $\alpha\in \sigma_{nl}(\bar c',X_{m'}^{\bar c'})$. Moreover, recall that $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})\subseteq \sigma'$. This gives 
    $$\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})\sqcup \{\alpha\}\subseteq \sigma_{nl}(\bar c',X_{m'}^{\bar c'})$$
    which contradicts the maximality of $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$. Therefore, $N=0$.
    
    To sum up, take $b\in\mathcal{C}_{leaf}$, $\mathcal{B}=\omega^{-1}\left(\langle {T_b} \rangle\right)$ and $W$ a Borel subset on which the canonical multicurve $\beta$ of $(\mathcal{B}|_W,\rho)$ exists. The curve $b$ can be completed into a multicurve of $D$ by Proposition~\ref{prop D les decompos particulieres avec g+p/3}. The fact that $N=0$ ensures that $\beta$ contains $0$ non-leaf curves. Claim~\ref{claim at most one leaf curve} ensures that $\beta$ is therefore reduced to exactly one leaf curve. Point~\ref{ii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} ensures that $(\mathcal{B},\rho)$ is trivial on $\Sigma\setminus \beta$, which concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- claim-2: body_md -->
\label{claim at most one leaf curve}
   Assume that $Y$ is a Borel subset of positive measure such that the canonical multicurve $\beta$ of $(\mathcal{B}|_Y,\rho)$ exists. Then, $\beta$ contains at most one leaf curve.
<!-- end claim-2 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Any leaf curve can be completed into a $0$-maximal decomposition, see for instance the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop D les decompos particulieres avec g+p/3} for a concrete construction. Apply Point~\ref{ii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} to get that $\mathcal{B}|_{Z}$ is sent by $\rho$ into a multitwist for a small enough positive measure subset $Z\subseteq Y$. In other words, $(\mathcal{B}|_Z,\rho)$ is trivial on every connected component of $\Sigma\setminus \beta$, so $$\rho\left(\mathcal{B}|_Z\right)\subseteq \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\beta}~.$$
          
    First, let us build everything we need on the surface at the source of the measurable embedding. Complete $b$ into a $0$-maximal decomposition $(b_1,\dots,b_{n-1})$ where $b_1=b$ and denote $S_1,\dots,S_n$ the components of this decomposition, where $S_1$ is an open leaf subsurface separated from $\Sigma$ by $b$. Recall that this means that $n={n_{\Sigma}}$ and all the curves $b_j$ are separating. Now, complete the leaf curve $b$ into another $0$-maximal decomposition $(b_1',b_2',\dots,b_{n-1}')$ where $b_1'=b$ and such that for all $i,j\ge 2$, $b_j'$ intersects the open subsurface $S_i$ nontrivially. Denote $\mathcal{B}_j=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{b_j}} \rangle)$ and $\mathcal{B}_j'=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{b'_j}} \rangle)$.

    Now, let us give notations to what happens on the target copy of $\Sigma$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}, restrict to a small enough Borel subset $U$ of positive measure, and denote by $\sigma$ (resp. $\sigma'$) the $0$-maximal decomposition multicurve at the target that we get from the decomposition $(b_j)_j$ (resp. $(b_j')_j$). For every $i\leq n$, denote by $Q_i$ the component of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma$ on which $\omega^{-1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{ S_i }(\Sigma))|_U$ is irreducible nonamenable. Similarly, $Q_i'$ is the component of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma'$ on which $\left(\omega^{-1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{ S_i' }(\Sigma))|_U,\rho\right)$ is irreducible nonamenable.
    Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma b intersects S} to $b=b_j'$, $S=S_i$ and $Q=Q_i$ to obtain that $(\mathcal{B}_j'|_U,\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q_i$ for every $2\leq i\leq n$ and $2\leq j \leq n-1$.
    
    Assume by contradiction that $\beta$ contains two distinct leaf curves $a$ and $a'$. The multicurve $\beta$ is contained in both $\sigma$ and $\sigma'$, so $\{a,a'\}\subseteq \sigma\cap\sigma'$. This means that there are two leaf subsurfaces of the target $\Sigma$ that are a component in both decompositions. One of them is $Q_1=Q_1'$ coming from $S_1=S_1'$. But there is a second one, so there exist $i,i'\geq 2$ such that $\partial Q_i=\partial Q_{i'}'$ is a leaf curve of $\beta$, which forces the leaf subsurfaces $Q_i$ and $Q_{i'}'$ to be equal. For $j\geq 2$, by Point~\ref{ii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo},
    $$\rho\left(\mathcal{B}_j'|_U\right)\subseteq \langle {T_{c}} \rangle_{c\in\sigma'}\subseteq \mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q_{i'}' }^*}(\Sigma)$$
    and $Q_i=Q_{i'}'$ so $$\mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q_{i} }^*}(\Sigma)=\mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q_{i'}' }^*}(\Sigma)~.$$
    Therefore, 
    $$\rho\left(\mathcal{B}_j'|_U\right)\subseteq \mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q_{i} }^*}(\Sigma)$$
    which contradicts the fact that $(\mathcal{B}_j'|_U,\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q_i$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- claim-3: body_md -->
\label{claim g_j' at least two curves}
        For $j>N$, $\gamma_j'$ contains at least two distinct curves in $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma\setminus \sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c}))$.
<!-- end claim-3 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Fix $j>N$. To prove this, we show that $\gamma_j'$ has curves on at least two distinct connected components of $\Sigma\setminus \sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$. Consider the dual tree $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ of the decomposition $\sigma$ as defined in Definition~\ref{def dual graph}. The edges of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ correspond to curves of $\sigma$. Let $E_{nl}$ be the edges of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ corresponding to $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$ and note that $\#E_{nl}=N$. Let $\Gamma_1,\dots,\Gamma_{N+1}$ be the connected components of the subforest of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ with vertices $V(\Gamma_{\sigma})$ and edges $E(\Gamma_{\sigma})\setminus E_{nl}$. Let us bound the size of these connected components. The tree $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ has at least two leaves, so up to renumbering, $\Gamma_1$ and $\Gamma_2$ contain a leaf. But $E_{nl}$ contains no edges that connect to a leaf, so $\#V(\Gamma_1),V(\Gamma_2)\geq 2$. The rest of the connected components verify $\#V(\Gamma_i)\geq 1$, which gives
    \begin{align*}
        \#V(\Gamma_1)&\leq \#V(\Gamma) - \#V(\Gamma_2)-\sum_{j=3}^{N+1} \#V(\Gamma_j)\\
                &\leq n -2 -\sum_{j=3}^{N+1} 1\\
                &\leq n-2-(N-1) = n-(N+1)
    \end{align*}
    and similarly $ \#V(\Gamma_2)\leq n-(N+1)$.
    For $i\leq 3$, it holds that
    \begin{align*}
        \#V(\Gamma_i)&\leq \#V(\Gamma) -\#V(\Gamma_1)- \#V(\Gamma_2)-\sum_{\substack{3\le j\le N+1\\ j\ne i}} \#V(\Gamma_j)\\
                &\leq n -2-2 -(N-2)\\
                &\leq n-(N+1)
    \end{align*}
    In conlusion, every connected component of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ with erased $E_{nl}$ edges has at most $n-(N+1)$ vertices.

    By Point~\ref{iii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}, the graph isomorphism $f_{\bar c,\sigma}$ sends the vertices $S_{N+1},\dots,S_n$ to vertices $Q_{N+1},\dots,Q_n$ inside of ${\Sigma \setminus\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})}$. In the point of view of dual graphs, this means exactly that $f_{\bar c,\sigma}$ is sending $n-(N+1)+1$ vertices of $\Gamma_{\bar c}$ into the subforest of $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ with erased $E_{nl}$ edges. We just showed that each connected component of that subforest has at most $n-(N+1)$ vertices. Thus, there are two vertices sent by $f_{\bar c,\sigma}$ to two different connected components of the forest. This means that, up to renumbering, $Q_{N+1}$ and $Q_{N+2}$ are in different connected components of $\Sigma\setminus\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$. But $\gamma_j'$ is disjoint from $\sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c})$ and nontrivial on both $Q_{N+1}$ and $Q_{N+2}$. Therefore, $\gamma_j'$ contains at least two distinct curves that are in $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma\setminus \sigma_{nl}(\bar c,X_m^{\bar c}))$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- prop-11: body_md -->
\label{nonsep curves prop}
Under Setting~\ref{setting for thm}, assume that $\Sigma=\Sigma'$ and $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 2$. Let $a\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ be a weakly separating or nonseparating curve. Let $\mathcal{A}=\omega^{-1}\left(\langle {T_a} \rangle\right)$. 

Then, there exists a countable conull Borel partition $\bigsqcup_m X_m$ of $X$ such that for each $m$, there exists a curve $\alpha_m\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ such that 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{A}|_{X_m})\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha_m}} \rangle~.$$
<!-- end prop-11 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
\textbf{Case 1: $\kappa(\Sigma)=2$.}
Let $\mathcal{H}=\omega^{-1}\left(\mathrm{Stab}(a)\right)$. The following is the only information we will use on the action-type cocycle $\omega$:
\begin{itemize}[nolistsep, noitemsep]
    \item $\mathcal{H}$ and $\mathcal{A}$ are of infinite type by Lemma~\ref{cocycles from pmp actions are action-type},
    \item $\mathcal{A}$ is amenable by Lemma~\ref{lemma triv kernel cocycle amenable group amenable groupoid},
    \item $\mathcal{H}$ is everywhere nonamenable by Lemma~\ref{lemme action-type groupe non moyennable donne groupoide non moyennable},
    \item $\mathcal{H}$ and $\mathcal{A}$ normalise each other by Lemma~\ref{lemma if groups normalise then groupoids normalise}.
\end{itemize}
 Now, focus on the cocycle $\rho$. Choose a countable conull Borel partition $\bigsqcup_m X_m$ adapted to the canonical reduction sets of both $(\mathcal{A},\rho)$ and $(\mathcal{H},\rho)$, by Lemma~\ref{canonical reduction set exists} and Remark~\ref{rem we can always choose U for the canonical multicurve to exist}. Take $U$ a piece of that partition of positive measure and let $\mathcal{H}'=\mathcal{H}|_U$ and $\mathcal{A}'=\mathcal{A}|_U$.

By Corollary~\ref{if IA or T everywhere, then amen. If nonamen then IN somewhere}, $\mathcal{H}'$ must be irreducible nonamenable on at least some component $Q$ of $\Sigma\setminus\sigma$, where $\sigma$ is the canonical reduction multicurve of $(\mathcal{H}',\rho)$. In particular, $\kappa(Q)\geq 1$ because $\mathcal{H}'$ is nontrivial on $Q$. 
The subgroupoid $\mathcal{A}'$ normalises $\mathcal{H}'$ so $(\mathcal{A}',\rho)$ stabilises $Q$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma multicurve is canonical}. Assume by contradiction that $(\mathcal{A}',\rho)$ is nontrivial on $Q$. Then, Proposition~\ref{if H normalises A then its reducible or IA} applied to $\mathcal{H}'$ normalising the amenable $\mathcal{A}'$ contradicts the fact that $(\mathcal{H}',\rho)$ is irreducible nonamenable on $Q$. Thus, $(\mathcal{A}',\rho)$ is trivial on $Q$, i.e.\ $$\rho(\mathcal{A}')\subseteq \mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q }^*}(\Sigma)~.$$

The case where $Q=\Sigma$ is impossible because the trivial-kernel cocycle $\rho$ cannot send the infinite-type groupoid $\mathcal{A}'$ into the trivial group, by Remark~\ref{rem trivial kernel infinite-type groupoid to infinite group}. Hence $\kappa(Q)=1$ and $\partial Q$ is composed of exactly one curve $\alpha$. In conclusion, 
$$\rho(\mathcal{A}')\subseteq \mathrm{Mod}_{ {Q }^*}(\Sigma)\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha}} \rangle$$
which proves the proposition in the case $\kappa(\Sigma)=2$.

\medbreak
\textbf{Case 2: $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 3$.} In this case, ${n_{\Sigma}}\geq 2$, i.e.\ the $0$-maximal decompositions of $\Sigma$ have at least $2$ components. In particular, leaf curves are strongly separating.

If $a$ is weakly separating, let $S$ be the interior of a subsurface of $\Sigma$ of type $\Sigma_{0,3,1}$ which contains $a$. If $a$ is nonseparating, let $S$ be the interior of a subsurface of type $\Sigma_{1,0,1}$ containing $a$. 
    In either case, $S$ is a leaf subsurface of complexity $1$, the boundary of $S$ is exactly one leaf curve $b=\partial S$, and $a\in\mathcal{C}(S)$. Complete $b$ into a $0$-maximal decomposition $S_1, S_2,\dots,S_n$ of $\Sigma$, with $S=S_1$ and $n={n_{\Sigma}}$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} and restrict to a small enough Borel subset $U$ of positive measure accordingly. Name $Q_1,\dots,Q_n$ the image decomposition components and $\sigma$ the decomposition multicurve on $U$. Let $\mathcal{A}=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{a}} \rangle)$ and note that 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{A}|_U)\subseteq \rho(\omega^{-1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{ S_1 }(\Sigma))|_U)\subseteq \mathrm{Mod}_{ Q_1 }(\Sigma)\times \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma\setminus \partial Q_1}~. $$
    Let $V$ be a positive measure Borel subset of $U$ on which the canonical multicurve $\alpha$ of $(\mathcal{A}|_V,\rho)$ exists. It is nonempty because $(\mathcal{A}|_V,\rho)$ is trivial for instance on $Q_2$. Note that $Q_2$ is well-defined because ${n_{\Sigma}}\geq 2$. By the latter, $\alpha$ contains only curves that are either in $\sigma$ or in $\mathcal{C}(Q_1)$.

    Take $b'$ a leaf curve that is disjoint from $a$ and intersects every $S_i$ nontrivially for $1\le i\le n$. 
    For instance, $b'$ can be taken as an image of $b$ by a pseudo-Anosov on $\Sigma\setminus a$. 
    Then, by Proposition~\ref{separating curves prop}, up to restricting to a smaller positive measure Borel subset $W\subseteq V$, the canonical multicurve of $\mathcal{B}'=\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{b'}} \rangle)|_W$ exists and is exactly one curve $\beta'$. By Lemma~\ref{lemma b intersects S}, this curve $\beta'$ intersects every $Q_i$ nontrivially for $1\le i\le n$. In particular, $\beta'$ being one unique curve, it has to intersect every curve of $\sigma$.

    The subgroupoid $\mathcal{B}'$ normalises $\mathcal{A}|_W$ so $(\mathcal{B}',\rho)$ stabilises $\alpha$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma multicurve is canonical}. Thus,
    $$\rho(\mathcal{B}')\subseteq \mathrm{Stab}(\alpha)$$
    but also by Proposition~\ref{separating curves prop},
    $$\rho(\mathcal{B}')\subseteq \langle {T_{\beta'}} \rangle$$
    which gives $$\rho(\mathcal{B}')\subseteq \mathrm{Stab}(\alpha)\cap\langle {T_{\beta'}} \rangle~.$$
    The groupoid $\mathcal{B}'$ being of infinite type and the cocycle $\rho$ having trivial kernel, Remark~\ref{rem trivial kernel infinite-type groupoid to infinite group} forces $\mathrm{Stab}(\alpha)\cap\langle {T_{\beta'}} \rangle$ to be an infinite group. Thus, there is a nontrivial power $k$ of $T_{\beta'}$ that stabilises the multicurve $\alpha$. As we are in the torsion-free $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$, $T_{\beta'}^k$ actually stabilises each curve of $\alpha$ which means that $\beta'$ and $\alpha$ are disjoint by Point~\ref{intersection formula dehn twists} of Proposition~\ref{denh twist prop}. But $\beta'$ intersects every curve of $\sigma$, so $\alpha\cap\sigma=\varnothing$. Thus, $\alpha\subseteq \mathcal{C}(Q_1)$.

    Remember that $\kappa(Q_1)=1$ by Point~\ref{i max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}, so any multicurve of $\mathcal{C}(Q_1)$ can only be one single curve, so $\alpha$ is one single curve of $\mathcal{C}(Q_1)$. So the groupoid $(\mathcal{A}|_W,\rho)$ is trivial on $\Sigma\setminus Q_1$ and stabilises a curve $\alpha\in\mathcal{C}(Q_1)$, i.e.\
    $$\rho(\mathcal{A}|_W)\subseteq \mathrm{Mod}_{ Q_1 }(\Sigma)\cap \mathrm{Stab}(\alpha)~.$$
    Note that $\kappa(Q_1)=1$, so $\kappa(Q_1\setminus \alpha)=0$, i.e.\ $Q_1\setminus\alpha$ is a pair of pants. Thus,
    $$\mathrm{Mod}_{ Q_1 }(\Sigma)\cap \mathrm{Stab}(\alpha)\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha},T_{\partial Q_1}} \rangle$$
    which gives 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{A}|_W)\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha},T_{\partial Q_1}} \rangle~.$$
    If $\rho(\mathcal{A}|_W)$ had a nontrivial intersection with $\langle {T_{\partial Q_1}} \rangle$, then the curve $\partial Q_1$ would be in the canonical multicurve of $(\mathcal{A}|_W,\rho)$. But the canonical multicurve of $(\mathcal{A}|_W,\rho)$ is the single curve $\alpha\in\mathcal{C}(Q_1)$. Thus, $$\rho(\mathcal{A}|_W)\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha}} \rangle$$
    which concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- prop-12: body_md -->
\label{separating curves prop}
Under Setting~\ref{setting for thm}, assume that $\Sigma=\Sigma'$. Let $a\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ be a strongly separating curve. Let $\mathcal{A}=\omega^{-1}\left(\langle {T_a} \rangle\right)$. Then, there exists a countable conull Borel partition $\bigsqcup_m X_m$ of $X$ such that for each $m$, there exists a curve $\alpha_m\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ such that 
    $$\rho(\mathcal{A}|_{X_m})\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha_m}} \rangle~.$$
<!-- end prop-12 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
If $a$ is a leaf curve, the previous Proposition~\ref{prop leaf type curves} applies. Now assume $a$ is not a leaf curve, and complete $a$ into a $k$-maximal decomposition $\bar a$ for $k$ either $0$ or $1$, by Lemma~\ref{lemma strongly sep curves into decompo}. Denote by $S_1,\dots,S_n$ the components of the decomposition $\bar a$, with $n={n_{\Sigma}} -k$. Number the $(S_i)_i$ so that $S_1,\dots,S_p$ are on one side of $a$ and $S_{p+1},\dots,S_n$ are on the other side of $a$, for some $p<n$.

    Now, let $b$ be a leaf curve that is disjoint from $a$ and that intersects every $S_i$ nontrivially for $1\leq i\leq p$. The curve $b$ exists because $a$ is not a leaf curve. Similarly, let $b'$ be a leaf curve disjoint from $a$ and that intersects $S_i$ nontrivially for $p+1\leq i\leq n$. Denote by $\mathcal{B}=\omega^{-1}\left(\langle {T_b} \rangle\right)$ and $\mathcal{B}'=\omega^{-1}\left(\langle {T_{b'}} \rangle\right)$. Note that $\mathcal{A}$ normalises $\mathcal{B}$ and $\mathcal{B}'$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma if groups normalise then groupoids normalise}, because the curves $a,b$ and $b'$ are disjoint.

    Apply Proposition~\ref{prop leaf type curves} to both $b$ and $b'$ and apply Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} to the decomposition $\bar a$. Take a conull Borel partition of $X$ that refines the three partitions given by the latter. Thus, for any piece $U$ of the partition,
    \begin{itemize}
    \item by Proposition~\ref{prop leaf type curves}, there exist two leaf curves $\beta$ and $\beta'$ such that $$\rho(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_b} \rangle)|_U)\subseteq \langle {T_{\beta}} \rangle~,$$
    $$\rho(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{b'}} \rangle)|_U)\subseteq \langle {T_{\beta'}} \rangle~;$$
    \item by Point~\ref{ii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}, there exists a $k$-maximal decomposition $\sigma$ of $\Sigma$ into components $Q_1,\dots,Q_n$ such that the canonical multicurve $\alpha$ of $(\mathcal{A}|_U,\rho)$ is constant, $\alpha\subseteq \sigma$ and $$\rho(\mathcal{A}|_U)\subseteq \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\alpha}~;$$
    \item by Points~\ref{i max decompos} and \ref{iii max decompos} of Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo}, the map $S_i\longmapsto Q_i$ is a graph isomorphism from $\Gamma_{\bar a}$ to $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ and for every $i\leq n$, $$\rho(\omega^{-1}(\mathrm{Mod}_{ S_i }(\Sigma))|_U)\subseteq\mathrm{Mod}_{ Q_i }(\Sigma)\times \langle {T_c} \rangle_{c\in\sigma\setminus \partial Q_i}~.$$
    \end{itemize}

    Let $c$ be the curve of $\sigma$ that is in the boundary of both $Q_p$ and $Q_{p+1}$. The graph $\Gamma_{\sigma}$ being a tree, this means that $c$ separates the components $(Q_i)_{i\leq p}$ from the components $(Q_i)_{i>p}$. 
    
    For $1\leq i\leq p$, apply Lemma~\ref{lemma b intersects S} to $b$ intersecting $S_i$ and obtain that $\beta$ intersects $Q_i$ nontrivially. Thus, the single curve $\beta$ intersects every $Q_i$ for $1\leq i\leq p$. Similarly, the single curve $\beta'$ intersects every $Q_i$ nontrivially for $p+1\leq i\leq n$. Therefore, each curve of $\sigma\setminus \{c\}$ intersects either $\beta$ or $\beta'$. Thus, the only curve of $\sigma$ that can be disjoint from both $\beta$ and $\beta'$ is $c$.

    The groupoid $\mathcal{A}$ normalises $\mathcal{B}$ (resp.\ $\mathcal{B}'$) so by Lemma~\ref{lemma multicurve is canonical}, $\alpha$ and $\beta$ (resp.\ $\beta'$) are disjoint. But remember that $\alpha\subseteq \sigma$. Therefore, $\alpha\subseteq \{c\}$, which concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- th-3: body_md -->
\label{irmak th}
    Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface of positive complexity. An injective map $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ that sends intersecting curves to intersecting curves and disjoint curves to disjoint curves is necessarily an automorphism of the graph $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$.
<!-- end th-3 -->
<!-- th-4: body_md -->
\label{thm existence of tautening map}
    Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface without boundary and with $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 2$. Let $H$ and $G$ be two copies of the group $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$. Let $\Omega$ be a measurable embedding coupling of $H$ into $G$. Then, there exists a measurable and $(G\times H)$-equivariant map
    $\Phi : \Omega \longrightarrow \operatorname{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$.
<!-- end th-4 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Fix a measurable embedding coupling $\Omega$ of $H$ into $G$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma measurable embedding provides groupoid and cocycles} to get $X=D_G\cap D_H$ where $D_G$ is a Borel fundamental domain for $G$ of finite measure and $D_H$ a Borel fundamental domain for $H$. These fundamental domains are chosen such that $(G\times H)X$ is conull in $\Omega$. The measured groupoid $\mathcal{G}=(H\ltimes D_G)|_X$ is equipped with two cocycles: $\omega : \mathcal{G} \longrightarrow H$ and $\rho : \mathcal{G} \longrightarrow G$. In other words, we are in Setting~\ref{setting for thm} with $\Sigma=\Sigma'$.
\medbreak
\textbf{Step 1.} Let us construct a measurable map $\theta : X ^*\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}{\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)}$ on some conull Borel subset $X^*$ of~$X$.

        \textit{$\drsh$ Definition of $\theta$.} 
    For $a\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$, by Proposition~\ref{prop recap courbes dans courbes}, there exists a countable conull Borel partition $X^a_*=\bigsqcup_{n}X_n^a$ of $X$ and $\alpha_n\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ such that for all $n$,
    $$\rho\left(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_a} \rangle)\right)|_{X_n^a}\subseteq \langle {T_{\alpha_n}} \rangle~.$$
    Then, define the conull Borel subset $$X^*=\bigcap_{a\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)} X^a_*$$
and define on $X^*$ the following map 
$$\theta : x\longmapsto \left( a\mapsto \alpha_n \text{ when }x\in X_n^a\right)~.$$


    Let us show that $\theta$ takes values in $\operatorname{Aut}{\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)}$. Fix $a,b,c\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ such that $a\neq b$ and $c$ intersects $a$ but is disjoint from $b$. Take a countable partition of $X ^*$ that refines the ones for $a,b$ and $c$. Take $x\in X^*$ and $U$ the piece of the partition that contains $x$. 
    
     \textit{$\drsh$ Adjacency of $\theta(x)$.}
   Apply Lemma~\ref{adjacency curves in groupoidal terms} to 
    $$\rho \left( \omega^{-1}(\langle {T_a} \rangle)|_U\right)\subseteq \langle {T_{\theta(x)(a)}} \rangle$$
    and 
    $$\rho \left(\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_b} \rangle)|_U\right)\subseteq  \langle {T_{\theta(x)(b)}} \rangle~.$$
    This gives us that $\theta(x)(a)$ and $\theta(x)(b)$ are adjacent in $\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ if and only if $a$ and $b$ are.

    \textit{$\drsh$ Injectivity of $\theta(x)$.}
    The adjacency property of $\theta$ guarantees that $\theta(x)(c)$ intersects $\theta(x)(a)$ but is disjoint from $\theta(x)(b)$. In particular, $\theta(x)(a)$ and $\theta(x)(b)$ are distinct vertices of the curve graph.

    We have shown that for every $x$ in $X^*$, $\theta(x)$ verifies the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{irmak th}, therefore it is an element of $\operatorname{Aut}{\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)}$.

\medbreak
\textbf{Step 2.} Now we show a form of $(\omega,\rho)$-equivariance of $\theta$. More precisely, let us show that for almost every $\mathfrak g\in\mathcal{G}$,
    \begin{align}\label{equivariance of theta}
        \rho(\mathfrak g)\theta(s(\mathfrak g)) =\theta(r(\mathfrak g))\omega(\mathfrak g)~.
    \end{align}
    Fix $g,h\in\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ and a bisection of positive measure $B$ on which $\rho$ is constant equal to $g$ and $\omega$ is constant equal to $h$.
We will show (\ref{equivariance of theta}) for every $\mathfrak g\in B$, which suffices because
$$\mathcal{G}=\bigsqcup_{g,h\in\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)} \omega^{-1}(\{h\})\cap \rho^{-1}(\{g\})~.$$

Fix $a\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$. Let $U$ a Borel subset of positive measure of the domain of $B$ small enough for $\alpha = \theta(x)(a)$ and $\beta=\theta(Bx)(ha)$ to be well defined and constant when $x$ varies in $U$. For $x\in U$, there is a unique $\mathfrak g\in B$ with source $x$ because $B$ is a bisection. Then, the range of $\mathfrak g$ is $Bx$, $\rho(\mathfrak g)=g$ and $\omega(\mathfrak g)=h$ so that
$$g\alpha =\rho(\mathfrak g)\theta(s(\mathfrak g))(a)$$
and
$$\beta=\theta(r(\mathfrak g))(\omega(\mathfrak g)a)~.$$Thus, to show (\ref{equivariance of theta}) it is now enough to show that $\beta=g\alpha$.

By definition of $\theta$, 
\begin{equation}\label{U}
    \omega^{-1}(\langle {T_a} \rangle)|_U\subseteq \rho^{-1}(\langle {T_{\alpha}} \rangle)|_U~,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{BU}
    \omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{ha}} \rangle)|_{BU}\subseteq \rho^{-1}(\langle {T_{\beta}} \rangle)|_{BU}~.
\end{equation}
Then, it holds that 
\begin{align*}
\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{ha}} \rangle)|_{BU}&=B\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_a} \rangle)|_U B^{-1}\\
                        &\subseteq B\rho^{-1}(\langle {T_{\alpha}} \rangle)|_U B^{-1}\text{  by }(\ref{U})\\
                        &=\rho^{-1}(\langle {T_{g\alpha}} \rangle)|_{BU}
\end{align*}
which then gives by $(\ref{BU})$
$$\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{ha}} \rangle)|_{BU}\subseteq \rho^{-1}(\langle {T_{\beta}} \rangle)|_{BU}\cap \rho^{-1}(\langle {T_{g\alpha}} \rangle)|_{BU}~.$$
The subgroupoid $\omega^{-1}(\langle {T_{ha}} \rangle)|_{BU}$ is of infinite type and $\rho$ has trivial kernel. By Remark~\ref{rem trivial kernel infinite-type groupoid to infinite group}, this forces the subgroup $\langle {T_{\beta}} \rangle\cap \langle {T_{g\alpha}} \rangle$ to be nontrivial, which means that $\beta=g\alpha$ by Point~\ref{dehn twists injectivity} of Proposition~\ref{denh twist prop}. This concludes the proof of $(\ref{equivariance of theta})$.
\medbreak
\textbf{Step 3.} Finally, we construct a tautening map on the whole space $\Omega$, i.e.\ a measurable map $$\Phi : \Omega \longrightarrow \operatorname{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$$
which is equivariant for the $G\times H$ action. More precisely, for every $g,h\in\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ and almost every $y\in\Omega$, 
$$\Phi((g,h)y)=g\Phi(y)h^{-1}~.$$

To construct $\Phi$, we extend $\theta$ by equivariance to the conull Borel subset $(G\times H)X^*$. For a fixed $y\in\Omega$, there exist $g,h\in G$ and $x\in X^*$ such that $y=(g,h)x$. Define
    $$\Phi((g,h)x)=g \theta(x)h^{-1}$$
    and note that it is automatically $(G\times H)$-equivariant. 
    It is left to show that $\Phi$ is well defined almost everywhere. Take $g_1,g_2\in G$, $h_1,h_2\in H$ and $x_1,x_2\in X^*$ such that 
    $$y=(g_1,h_1)x_1=(g_2,h_2)x_2~.$$
    Thus, for $g=g_1 ^{-1}g_2$ and $h=h_1^{-1}h_2$ it holds that $x_1=(g,h)x_2$. In particular, the element $$\mathfrak g\mathrel{\mathop:}= (g,x_2)=(h,x_2)$$ in the groupoid $\mathcal{G}$ has source $x_2$ and range $x_1$. Applying (\ref{equivariance of theta}) to $\mathfrak g$ and using that $\rho(\mathfrak g)=g$ and $\omega(\mathfrak g)=h$, we get 
    \begin{equation}\label{application of equivariance}
      g\theta(x_2)=\theta(x_1)h
    \end{equation}
    for almost every $x_1,x_2\in X^*$. Therefore, 
    \begin{align*}
        \Phi((g_1,h_1)x_1)&=g_1\theta(x_1)h_1^{-1}\\
                        &=g_1\left(g_1 ^{-1}g_2\right)\theta(x_2)\left(h_1 ^{-1}h_2\right)^{-1}h_1^{-1}\text{ by~(\ref{application of equivariance})}\\
                        &=g_2\theta(x_2)h_2^{-1}\\
                        &=\Phi((g_2,h_2)x_2)
    \end{align*}
    for almost every $x_1,x_2\in X$ as wanted.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- manualtheorem-1: body_md -->
{\ref{main thm}}
Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface without boundary and with $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 2$. Then, any measurable embedding coupling from $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ to $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ is necessarily a measure equivalence coupling.
<!-- end manualtheorem-1 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
As explained in Remark~\ref{remark finite indices in me couplings}, it suffices to study a measurable embedding of the finite-index subgroup $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ of $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ into itself. Hence, fix two copies $h$ and $G$ of $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ and fix $\Omega$ a measurable embedding coupling of $H$ into $G$. By Theorem~\ref{thm existence of tautening map}, there exists a measurable and $(G\times H)$-equivariant map ${\Phi : \Omega \longrightarrow \operatorname{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)}$.
    
    By Ivanov's Theorem~\ref{th ivanov}, $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ is a finite-index subgroup of $\operatorname{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$. This means that $H$ acts on $\operatorname{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$ with a finite fundamental domain $K$. The tautening map provides us with the Borel subset $\Phi^{-1}(K)$. It is a fundamental domain for $H$ acting on $\Omega$, and it has finite measure because $K$ is finite. This proves that $\Omega$ is a measure equivalence coupling.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- manualcor-1: body_md -->
{\ref{cr biembedability}}
Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface without boundary and with $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 2$. If a countable group $H$ measurably embeds into $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ and $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ measurably embeds into $H$, then $H$ is virtually isomorphic to $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ up to finite kernels.
<!-- end manualcor-1 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
By Theorem~\ref{main thm} and Proposition~\ref{transitivité du plongement ME}, $H$ is measure equivalent to $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$. By Kida's \cite[Theorem~1.1]{kida_measure_2010}, $H$ is virtually isomorphic to $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ up to finite kernels.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- manualcor-2: body_md -->
{\ref{cr monotonicity of complexity}}
    Let $\Sigma$ and $\Sigma'$ be connected surfaces without boundaries and with $\kappa(\Sigma),\kappa(\Sigma')\geq 0$. If $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ measurably embeds into $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma')$, then ${\kappa(\Sigma)\leq \kappa(\Sigma')}$.
<!-- end manualcor-2 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Assume that there is a measurable embedding from $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ to $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma')$.
    If $\kappa(\Sigma')=0$, then $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma')$ is amenable so $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$ is also amenable by Remark~\ref{rem amen is preserved by meas emb}, which means that $\kappa(\Sigma)=0$.
    Now, assume that $\kappa(\Sigma')\geq 1$. By Remark~\ref{remark finite indices in me couplings}, there is a measurable embedding from $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ to $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma')$ so we find ourselves in Setting~\ref{setting for thm}. Thus, Lemma~\ref{lemma max decompo are sent to max decompo} applies and gives ${\kappa(\Sigma)\leq \kappa(\Sigma')}$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- manualcor-3: body_md -->
{\ref{cr OE rigidity}}Let $\Sigma$ be a connected surface without boundary and with $\kappa(\Sigma)\geq 2$ and let $G$ and $H$ be two copies of $\mathrm{Mod}(\Sigma)$. Assume that there exist free probability measure-preserving actions of $G$ and $H$ on a standard probability space $(X,\mu)$ such that for almost every $x\in X$, $H\cdot x\subseteq G\cdot x$. 
 
Then, for almost every $x\in X$, $H\cdot x=G\cdot x$. If additionally the actions are ergodic, then they are virtually conjugate.
<!-- end manualcor-3 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
First, let us construct a measurable embedding coupling of $H$ into $G$ from this context. This argument can be found in \cite[Remark~2.36]{delabie_quantitative_2022} with $X=Y$ and in \cite[Proposition~2.5]{demir_measurable_2022}. 
 Let
 $$\Omega =\{ (x,y)\in X\times X\mid y\in G\cdot x\}$$
 the countable Borel equivalence relation associated to the action of $G$ on $X$. It is a standard measured space equipped with the usual measure denoted $m$ and defined in \cite{feldman_ergodic_1977}. 
 Now, for $(x,y)\in\Omega$, $g\in G$ and $h\in H$, let
 $$g(x,y)=(x,g\cdot y)\text{ and }h(x,y)=(h\cdot x,y)~.$$
 This defines two free commuting measure-preserving actions of $G$ and $H$ on $(\Omega,m)$. To be clear, the p.m.p.\ actions on $X$ are denoted by $\cdot$ and the commuting actions on the space $\Omega$ of infinite measure have no notation. 

 Now, the Borel subset
 $$\Delta =\{(x,x)\mid x\in X\}$$
of $\Omega$ has finite measure $m(\Delta)=\mu(X)$. On one hand, $\Delta$ is a fundamental domain for the action of $G$ on $\Omega$. On the other hand, the action of $H$ on $X$ is free, so for every $h,h'\in H$, $h\Delta$ and $h'\Delta$ are disjoint. This provides a way to construct a Borel fundamental domain for the action of $H$ on $\Omega$ that contains $\Delta$. For the detailed construction, see \cite[Proposition~2.5]{demir_measurable_2022}. In particular, $\Omega$ is a measurable embedding coupling of $H$ into $G$. 

\begin{Claim}\label{claim HDelta is conull in Omega}
    The Borel subset $H\Delta$ is conull in $\Omega$.
\end{Claim}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Claim~\ref{claim HDelta is conull in Omega}]
Let $G^0\leq G$ and $H^0\leq H$ be two copies of $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ and denote $n$ the finite index of these subgroups. By Remark~\ref{remark finite indices in me couplings}, $\Omega$ is a measurable embedding coupling of $H^0$ into $G^0$. As in that remark, fix finite systems of representatives $R_H$ and $R_G$ for $H^0\leq H$ and $G^0\leq G$. On one hand, $R_G\Delta$ is a fundamental domain for $G^0$ acting on $\Omega$ and $m(R_G\Delta)=nm(\Delta)$. On the other hand, for every $h,h'\in H^0$, $h R_H\Delta$ and $h' R_H\Delta$ are disjoint.

Apply Theorem~\ref{thm existence of tautening map} to the measurable embedding coupling $\Omega$ of $H^0$ into $G^0$. There exists a measurable and $(G^0\times H^0)$-equivariant map $\Phi : \Omega\longrightarrow \operatorname{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$. Recall that $G^0\times H^0$ acts by left and right translations on $\operatorname{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$, so there exists a common finite fundamental domain $K$ for these two actions. Then, the Borel subset $$D=\Phi^{-1}(K)$$
of $\Omega$ is a common fundamental domain for the actions of $G^0$ and $H^0$ on $\Omega$.

Note that any two Borel fundamental domains for the action of $G^0$ (resp. $H^0$) on $\Omega$ have the same measure. 
Both $D$ and $R_G\Delta$ are Borel fundamental domains for $G^0$ acting on $\Omega$, so $$m(D)=m(R_G\Delta)=nm(\Delta)~.$$
The $H^0$-translates of $R_H\Delta$ are all pairwise disjoint, so $$R_H\Delta \sqcup \left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)$$ is a Borel fundamental domain for $H^0$ acting on $\Omega$. Hence, this subset has the same measure as $D$, so
\begin{align*}
    m(D)&=m\left(R_H\Delta \sqcup \left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)\right)\\
        &=m(R_H\Delta)+m\left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)\\
        &=nm(\Delta)+m\left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)\\
        &=m(D)+m\left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)~.
\end{align*}
This gives $m\left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)= 0$.

Recall that $H^0R_H=H$ so that $D\setminus H\Delta$ has null measure. Thus, the Borel subset
$$H\left(D\setminus H\Delta\right) = \Omega\setminus H\Delta$$
of $\Omega$ also has null measure. In conclusion, $H\Delta$ is conull in $\Omega$. 
\end{proof}

\begin{Claim}\label{claim almost every x}
For almost every $x\in X$ and every $g\in G$, $g(x,x)\in H\Delta$.
\end{Claim}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Claim~\ref{claim almost every x}]
    Take $g\in G$ and a Borel subset $U$ of $X$ such that for every $x\in U$, $g(x,x)\notin H\Delta$. Denote $\Delta_U=\{(x,x)\}_{x\in U}$ and note that $m(\Delta_U)=\mu(U)$. The latter gives $g\Delta_U\subseteq \Omega\setminus H\Delta$. By Claim~\ref{claim HDelta is conull in Omega}, $m(\Delta_U)= 0$ so $\mu(U)=0$.
\end{proof}

Let us now show that for almost every $x\in X$, $G\cdot x\subseteq H\cdot x$. 
Take $g\in G$ and $x\in X$ such that $g(x,x)\in H\Delta$. Then, there exists $h\in H$ and $y\in X$ such that $g(x,x)=h(y,y)$. By definition of the actions on $\Omega$, we get $g\cdot x=y$ and $h\cdot y=x$. Thus, $g\cdot x=h^{-1}\cdot x\in H\cdot x$ as wanted. The latter holds for almost every $x$ by Claim~\ref{claim almost every x}.

Assume additionnally that the actions of $G$ and $H$ on $X$ are ergodic. By \cite[Theorem~1.1]{kida_orbit_2008}, these actions are virtually conjugate.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- claim-4: body_md -->
\label{claim HDelta is conull in Omega}
    The Borel subset $H\Delta$ is conull in $\Omega$.
<!-- end claim-4 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Let $G^0\leq G$ and $H^0\leq H$ be two copies of $\mathrm{Mod}^0(\Sigma)$ and denote $n$ the finite index of these subgroups. By Remark~\ref{remark finite indices in me couplings}, $\Omega$ is a measurable embedding coupling of $H^0$ into $G^0$. As in that remark, fix finite systems of representatives $R_H$ and $R_G$ for $H^0\leq H$ and $G^0\leq G$. On one hand, $R_G\Delta$ is a fundamental domain for $G^0$ acting on $\Omega$ and $m(R_G\Delta)=nm(\Delta)$. On the other hand, for every $h,h'\in H^0$, $h R_H\Delta$ and $h' R_H\Delta$ are disjoint.

Apply Theorem~\ref{thm existence of tautening map} to the measurable embedding coupling $\Omega$ of $H^0$ into $G^0$. There exists a measurable and $(G^0\times H^0)$-equivariant map $\Phi : \Omega\longrightarrow \operatorname{Aut}\,\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)$. Then, the Borel subset $$D=\Phi^{-1}(K)$$
of $\Omega$ is a common fundamental domain for the actions of $G^0$ and $H^0$ on $\Omega$.

Note that any two Borel fundamental domains for the action of $G^0$ (resp. $H^0$) on $\Omega$ have the same measure. 
Both $D$ and $R_G\Delta$ are Borel fundamental domains for $G^0$ acting on $\Omega$, so $$m(D)=m(R_G\Delta)=nm(\Delta)~.$$
The $H^0$-translates of $R_H\Delta$ are all pairwise disjoint, so $$R_H\Delta \sqcup \left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)$$ is a Borel fundamental domain for $H^0$ acting on $\Omega$. Hence, this subset has the same measure as $D$, so
\begin{align*}
    m(D)&=m\left(R_H\Delta \sqcup \left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)\right)\\
        &=m(R_H\Delta)+m\left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)\\
        &=nm(\Delta)+m\left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)\\
        &=m(D)+m\left(D\setminus H^0R_H\Delta\right)~.

Recall that $H^0R_H=H$ so that $D\setminus H\Delta$ has null measure. Thus, the Borel subset
$$H\left(D\setminus H\Delta\right) = \Omega\setminus H\Delta$$
of $\Omega$ also has null measure. In conclusion, $H\Delta$ is conull in $\Omega$.

<!-- claim-5: body_md -->
\label{claim almost every x}
For almost every $x\in X$ and every $g\in G$, $g(x,x)\in H\Delta$.
<!-- end claim-5 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Take $g\in G$ and a Borel subset $U$ of $X$ such that for every $x\in U$, $g(x,x)\notin H\Delta$. Denote $\Delta_U=\{(x,x)\}_{x\in U}$ and note that $m(\Delta_U)=\mu(U)$. The latter gives $g\Delta_U\subseteq \Omega\setminus H\Delta$. By Claim~\ref{claim HDelta is conull in Omega}, $m(\Delta_U)= 0$ so $\mu(U)=0$.
